# CNCF Projects: Networking, Security, and Observability

This page covers the CNCF and three of its projects, Istio, Falco, and the Open Policy Agent (OPA), plus Gatekeeper. The practice run needs no cluster, root rights, or cloud account: `istioctl` and `gator` check local files, OPA runs locally, and Falco reads audit events from a file. It follows [CNCF Projects: Runtime, Scheduling, and Data](/devops/cncf-runtime-data/). For tools that collect metrics, logs, and traces, see [Monitoring and Observability](/devops/monitoring-observability/).

<PageMeta />

## What it is

<Voices>
<Technical>

**The CNCF and its stages.** The Cloud Native Computing Foundation (CNCF) hosts critical components of the global technology infrastructure <Cite n={1} />. CNCF is part of the nonprofit Linux Foundation <Cite n={1} />. Its Technical Oversight Committee (TOC) defines 4 stages of the project lifecycle <Cite n={3} />. The CNCF projects page says that graduated and incubating projects are considered stable and are used successfully in production environments <Cite n={2} />. The TOC describes Incubation projects as projects gaining adoption, focusing on improving stability and maturity <Cite n={3} />. It describes Graduated projects as highly mature, robust projects whose adopters have demonstrated their production-readiness by their deployment to production environments <Cite n={3} />.

**The three projects.** The CNCF page says that Istio was accepted on September 30, 2022 at the Incubating maturity level <Cite n={4} />. Istio moved to the Graduated maturity level on July 12, 2023 <Cite n={4} />. The CNCF Falco page says that Falco was accepted on October 10, 2018 <Cite n={5} />. Falco moved to the Incubating maturity level on January 8, 2020 <Cite n={5} />. Falco moved to the Graduated maturity level on February 29, 2024 <Cite n={5} />. The CNCF OPA page says that the Open Policy Agent (OPA) was accepted on March 29, 2018 <Cite n={6} />. OPA moved to the Incubating maturity level on April 2, 2019 <Cite n={6} />. OPA moved to the Graduated maturity level on January 29, 2021 <Cite n={6} />. The project pages describe Istio as a service mesh that simplifies observability, traffic management, security, and policy <Cite n={4} />. They describe Falco as the cloud-native runtime security project <Cite n={5} />, and OPA as an open source, general-purpose policy engine <Cite n={6} />.

**Istio: a service mesh.** The Istio docs define a service mesh as an infrastructure layer that gives applications capabilities like zero-trust security, observability, and advanced traffic management, without code changes <Cite n={7} />. Istio is an open source service mesh that layers transparently onto existing distributed applications <Cite n={8} />. It uses a proxy to intercept all your network traffic, which allows a broad set of application-aware features based on configuration you set <Cite n={8} />. The control plane takes your desired configuration, and its view of the services, and dynamically programs the proxy servers <Cite n={8} />. Istio gives you secure service-to-service communication in a cluster with mutual TLS encryption <Cite n={8} />. It gives you fine-grained control of traffic behavior with rich routing rules, retries, failovers, and fault injection <Cite n={8} />. It also gives you automatic metrics, logs, and traces for all traffic within a cluster <Cite n={8} />.

**Istio: two data plane modes.** Istio supports two data plane modes <Cite n={8} />. Sidecar mode deploys an Envoy proxy along with each pod that you start in your cluster, or running alongside services running on VMs <Cite n={8} />. Ambient mode uses a per-node Layer 4 proxy, and optionally a per-namespace Envoy proxy for Layer 7 features <Cite n={8} />. In ambient mode, the ztunnel secure overlay handles routing and zero trust security for traffic <Cite n={10} />. When needed, users can enable L7 waypoint proxies to get access to the full range of Istio features <Cite n={10} />. Pods and workloads using sidecar mode can co-exist within the same mesh as pods that use ambient mode <Cite n={10} />.

**Falco: runtime security.** Falco is a cloud native security tool that provides runtime security across hosts, containers, Kubernetes, and cloud environments <Cite n={18} />. At its core, Falco is a monitoring and detection agent that observes events and delivers real-time alerts based on custom rules <Cite n={18} />. Falco also enhances these events by integrating contextual metadata from container runtimes and Kubernetes <Cite n={18} />. Falco uses syscalls to monitor a system's activity <Cite n={18} />. Falco ships with a default set of rules that check the kernel for unusual behavior <Cite n={18} />. One example of the unusual behavior is privilege escalation using privileged containers <Cite n={18} />.

**OPA: a policy engine.** The OPA docs say that OPA is an open source, general-purpose policy engine that unifies policy enforcement across the stack <Cite n={27} />. OPA provides a high-level declarative language that lets you specify policy as code <Cite n={27} />. OPA decouples policy decision-making from policy enforcement <Cite n={27} />. When your software needs to make policy decisions, it queries OPA and supplies structured data as input <Cite n={27} />. OPA generates policy decisions by evaluating the query input against policies and data <Cite n={27} />. Policy decisions are not limited to simple yes/no or allow/deny answers <Cite n={27} />.

**Gatekeeper: OPA for Kubernetes.** In Kubernetes, admission controllers enforce policies on objects during create, update, and delete operations <Cite n={34} />. OPA Gatekeeper is a specialized project providing first-class integration between OPA and Kubernetes <Cite n={34} />. The OPA docs say that Gatekeeper is the recommended project for using OPA for Kubernetes admission control <Cite n={34} />. Gatekeeper adds native Kubernetes CRDs for extending the policy library, which are called constraint templates <Cite n={34} />. It adds native Kubernetes CRDs for instantiating the policy library, which are called constraints <Cite n={34} />. ConstraintTemplates define a way to validate some set of Kubernetes objects in Gatekeeper's Kubernetes admission controller <Cite n={37} />.

**What the practice run covers.** This paragraph describes the tutorial's own run. The run shows one privileged Pod from three sides. OPA and Gatekeeper report it as a policy violation in a local test, and Falco raises an alert for it from an audit event. Istio has no such Pod in the run. For Istio, the run checks files that describe traffic and security with `istioctl analyze`, and it renders the install manifests of five profiles with `istioctl manifest generate`.

</Technical>

One example of the unusual behavior is privilege escalation using privileged containers <Cite n={18} />.

When your software needs to make policy decisions, it queries OPA and supplies structured data as input <Cite n={27} />. OPA generates policy decisions by evaluating the query input against policies and data <Cite n={27} />. Policy decisions are not limited to simple yes/no or allow/deny answers <Cite n={27} />.

ConstraintTemplates define a way to validate some set of Kubernetes objects in Gatekeeper's Kubernetes admission controller <Cite n={37} />.

<Bangla>

**CNCF আর তার ধাপ।** Cloud Native Computing Foundation (CNCF) বিশ্বব্যাপী প্রযুক্তি অবকাঠামোর গুরুত্বপূর্ণ (critical) অংশ host করে <Cite n={1} />। CNCF অলাভজনক Linux Foundation-এর অংশ <Cite n={1} />। তার Technical Oversight Committee (TOC) project জীবনচক্রের ৪টা ধাপ ঠিক করে <Cite n={3} />। CNCF-এর projects page বলে, graduated আর incubating project stable বলে গণ্য হয় আর production পরিবেশে সফলভাবে ব্যবহৃত হয় <Cite n={2} />। TOC Incubation project-কে বর্ণনা করে এমন project হিসেবে, যা গ্রহণযোগ্যতা পাচ্ছে আর স্থিতিশীলতা ও পরিপক্বতা বাড়ানোর দিকে মন দিচ্ছে <Cite n={3} />। সে Graduated project-কে বর্ণনা করে অত্যন্ত পরিপক্ব, মজবুত project হিসেবে, যাদের ব্যবহারকারীরা production পরিবেশে deploy করে তাদের production-প্রস্তুতি দেখিয়েছেন <Cite n={3} />।

**তিনটা project।** CNCF page বলে, Istio গ্রহণ করা হয়েছে ২০২২ সালের ৩০ সেপ্টেম্বর Incubating স্তরে <Cite n={4} />। Istio ২০২৩ সালের ১২ জুলাই Graduated স্তরে উঠেছে <Cite n={4} />। CNCF-এর Falco page বলে, Falco গ্রহণ করা হয়েছে ২০১৮ সালের ১০ অক্টোবর <Cite n={5} />। Falco Incubating স্তরে উঠেছে ২০২০ সালের ৮ জানুয়ারি <Cite n={5} />। Falco Graduated স্তরে উঠেছে ২০২৪ সালের ২৯ ফেব্রুয়ারি <Cite n={5} />। CNCF-এর OPA page বলে, Open Policy Agent (OPA) গ্রহণ করা হয়েছে ২০১৮ সালের ২৯ মার্চ <Cite n={6} />। OPA Incubating স্তরে উঠেছে ২০১৯ সালের ২ এপ্রিল <Cite n={6} />। OPA Graduated স্তরে উঠেছে ২০২১ সালের ২৯ জানুয়ারি <Cite n={6} />। project page-গুলো Istio-কে বর্ণনা করে এমন একটা service mesh হিসেবে, যা observability, traffic management, security আর policy সহজ করে <Cite n={4} />। তারা Falco-কে বর্ণনা করে cloud-native runtime security project হিসেবে <Cite n={5} />, আর OPA-কে একটা open source, general-purpose policy engine হিসেবে <Cite n={6} />।

**Istio: একটা service mesh।** Istio docs service mesh-কে সংজ্ঞায়িত করে এমন একটা অবকাঠামো স্তর হিসেবে, যা application-কে code না বদলিয়ে zero-trust security, observability আর advanced traffic management-এর মতো সামর্থ্য দেয় <Cite n={7} />। Istio একটা open source service mesh, যা বিদ্যমান distributed application-এর ওপর স্বচ্ছভাবে বসে <Cite n={8} />। সে একটা proxy দিয়ে আপনার সব network traffic আটকায়, যার ফলে আপনার ঠিক করা configuration-এর ভিত্তিতে application-সচেতন features-এর একটা বিস্তৃত সেট পাওয়া যায় <Cite n={8} />। control plane আপনার চাওয়া configuration আর service-গুলো সম্পর্কে তার ধারণা নিয়ে proxy server-গুলোকে গতিশীলভাবে program করে <Cite n={8} />। Istio আপনাকে দেয় একটা cluster-এর ভেতরে mutual TLS encryption-সহ নিরাপদ service-to-service যোগাযোগ <Cite n={8} />। সে দেয় সমৃদ্ধ routing rule, retry, failover আর fault injection দিয়ে traffic আচরণের সূক্ষ্ম নিয়ন্ত্রণ <Cite n={8} />। সে আরও দেয় একটা cluster-এর ভেতরের সব traffic-এর জন্য স্বয়ংক্রিয় metrics, logs আর traces <Cite n={8} />।

**Istio: দুটো data plane mode।** Istio দুটো data plane mode সমর্থন করে <Cite n={8} />। Sidecar mode আপনার cluster-এ চালু করা প্রতিটা pod-এর সঙ্গে একটা Envoy proxy বসায়, অথবা VM-এ চলা service-এর পাশে চালায় <Cite n={8} />। Ambient mode ব্যবহার করে প্রতি node-এ একটা Layer 4 proxy, আর ঐচ্ছিকভাবে Layer 7 features-এর জন্য প্রতি namespace-এ একটা Envoy proxy <Cite n={8} />। ambient mode-এ ztunnel secure overlay traffic-এর routing আর zero trust security সামলায় <Cite n={10} />। দরকার হলে ব্যবহারকারীরা Istio-র সব features পেতে L7 waypoint proxy চালু করতে পারেন <Cite n={10} />। sidecar mode-এর pod আর workload একই mesh-এ ambient mode-এর pod-এর সঙ্গে থাকতে পারে <Cite n={10} />।

**Falco: runtime security।** Falco একটা cloud native security tool, যা host, container, Kubernetes আর cloud পরিবেশ জুড়ে runtime security দেয় <Cite n={18} />। মূলে Falco একটা monitoring আর detection agent, যে event লক্ষ করে আর custom rule-এর ভিত্তিতে real-time alert দেয় <Cite n={18} />। Falco container runtime আর Kubernetes থেকে প্রাসঙ্গিক metadata যোগ করে এই event-গুলোকে আরও সমৃদ্ধ করে <Cite n={18} />। Falco একটা system-এর কার্যকলাপ নজরে রাখতে syscall ব্যবহার করে <Cite n={18} />। Falco-র সঙ্গে আসে default rule-এর একটা সেট, যা kernel-এ অস্বাভাবিক আচরণ খোঁজে <Cite n={18} />। অস্বাভাবিক আচরণের একটা উদাহরণ হলো privileged container দিয়ে privilege escalation <Cite n={18} />।

**OPA: একটা policy engine।** OPA docs বলে, OPA একটা open source, general-purpose policy engine, যা পুরো stack জুড়ে policy enforcement একীভূত করে <Cite n={27} />। OPA একটা উচ্চস্তরের declarative ভাষা দেয়, যাতে আপনি policy-কে code হিসেবে লিখতে পারেন <Cite n={27} />। OPA policy সিদ্ধান্ত নেওয়াকে policy প্রয়োগ থেকে আলাদা করে <Cite n={27} />। আপনার software-কে policy সিদ্ধান্ত নিতে হলে সে OPA-কে query করে আর structured data input হিসেবে দেয় <Cite n={27} />। OPA query input-কে policy আর data-র বিরুদ্ধে মূল্যায়ন করে policy সিদ্ধান্ত তৈরি করে <Cite n={27} />। policy সিদ্ধান্ত কেবল সাধারণ yes/no বা allow/deny উত্তরে সীমিত নয় <Cite n={27} />।

**Gatekeeper: Kubernetes-এর জন্য OPA।** Kubernetes-এ admission controller create, update আর delete operation-এর সময় object-এর ওপর policy প্রয়োগ করে <Cite n={34} />। OPA Gatekeeper একটা বিশেষায়িত project, যা OPA আর Kubernetes-এর মধ্যে first-class integration দেয় <Cite n={34} />। OPA docs বলে, Kubernetes admission control-এর জন্য OPA ব্যবহারে Gatekeeper-ই সুপারিশ করা project <Cite n={34} />। Gatekeeper policy library বাড়ানোর জন্য native Kubernetes CRD যোগ করে, যাদের বলা হয় constraint template <Cite n={34} />। সে policy library-কে instance বানানোর জন্য native Kubernetes CRD যোগ করে, যাদের বলা হয় constraint <Cite n={34} />। ConstraintTemplate Gatekeeper-এর Kubernetes admission controller-এ একগুচ্ছ Kubernetes object যাচাই করার একটা উপায় ঠিক করে <Cite n={37} />।

**practice run কী কী দেখায়।** এই অনুচ্ছেদ টিউটোরিয়ালের নিজের run বর্ণনা করে। run একটা privileged Pod-কে তিন দিক থেকে দেখায়। OPA আর Gatekeeper একটা স্থানীয় test-এ তাকে policy violation হিসেবে জানায়, আর Falco একটা audit event থেকে তার জন্য alert তোলে। run-এ Istio-র এমন কোনো Pod নেই। Istio-র জন্য run `istioctl analyze` দিয়ে traffic আর security বর্ণনা করা file পরীক্ষা করে, আর `istioctl manifest generate` দিয়ে পাঁচটা profile-এর install manifest বানায়।

</Bangla>
</Voices>

## How it works

<Voices>
<Technical>

**Istio: the two planes.** An Istio service mesh is logically split into a data plane and a control plane <Cite n={9} />. In sidecar mode, the data plane is a set of intelligent proxies, Envoy, deployed as sidecars <Cite n={9} />. These proxies mediate and control all network communication between microservices <Cite n={9} />. They also collect and report telemetry on all mesh traffic <Cite n={9} />. Envoy proxies are the only Istio components that interact with data plane traffic <Cite n={9} />. The control plane manages and configures the proxies to route traffic <Cite n={9} />.

**Istiod.** Istiod provides service discovery, configuration, and certificate management <Cite n={9} />. It converts high level routing rules that control traffic behavior into Envoy-specific configurations, and propagates them to the sidecars at runtime <Cite n={9} />. Istiod acts as a Certificate Authority (CA) and generates certificates to allow secure mTLS communication in the data plane <Cite n={9} />. The Istio docs say that the sidecar proxy model allows you to add Istio capabilities to an existing deployment without requiring you to rearchitect or rewrite code <Cite n={9} />.

**Ambient mode.** In ambient mode, Istio implements its features using a per-node Layer 4 proxy, and optionally a per-namespace Layer 7 proxy <Cite n={10} />. This layered approach lets you adopt Istio in a more incremental fashion <Cite n={10} />. Waypoint proxies are heavier than the ztunnel overlay alone <Cite n={10} />. They still run as an ambient component of the infrastructure, and they need no modification of the application pods <Cite n={10} />.

**Traffic routing: virtual services and destination rules.** Virtual services, along with destination rules, are the key building blocks of Istio's traffic routing functionality <Cite n={11} />. A virtual service lets you configure how requests are routed to a service within an Istio service mesh <Cite n={11} />. Each virtual service consists of a set of routing rules that are evaluated in order <Cite n={11} />. A virtual service can send traffic to different versions of a service, such as "20% of calls go to the new version" <Cite n={11} />. This allows a canary rollout, where you gradually increase the percentage of traffic that is sent to a new service version <Cite n={11} />. Destination rules let you customize Envoy's traffic policies when calling the entire destination service or a particular service subset <Cite n={11} />. In some cases you also need to configure destination rules to use the features of virtual services, as destination rules are where you specify your service subsets <Cite n={11} />.

**Gateways.** Gateways are primarily used to manage ingress traffic, but you can also configure egress gateways <Cite n={11} />. An egress gateway lets you configure a dedicated exit node for the traffic leaving the mesh <Cite n={11} />. Istio provides some preconfigured gateway proxy deployments that you can use, istio-ingressgateway and istio-egressgateway <Cite n={11} />. Both are deployed if you use the demo installation <Cite n={11} />. Just the ingress gateway is deployed with the default profile <Cite n={11} />.

**Resilience: timeouts, retries, and circuit breakers.** A timeout is the amount of time that an Envoy proxy should wait for replies from a given service <Cite n={11} />. The Envoy timeout for HTTP requests is disabled in Istio by default <Cite n={11} />. A retry setting specifies the maximum number of times an Envoy proxy attempts to connect to a service if the initial call fails <Cite n={11} />. The default retry behavior for HTTP requests is to retry twice before returning the error <Cite n={11} />. In a circuit breaker, you set limits for calls to individual hosts within a service <Cite n={11} />. Once that limit has been reached, the circuit breaker "trips" and stops further connections to that host <Cite n={11} />. You configure circuit breaker thresholds in destination rules <Cite n={11} />.

**Fault injection.** Fault injection is a testing method that introduces errors into a system to ensure that it can withstand and recover from error conditions <Cite n={11} />. Istio lets you inject faults at the application layer, unlike delaying packets or killing pods at the network layer <Cite n={11} />. You can inject two types of faults, both configured using a virtual service <Cite n={11} />. Delays are timing failures that mimic increased network latency or an overloaded upstream service <Cite n={11} />. Aborts are crash failures that mimic failures in upstream services <Cite n={11} />. Currently, the fault injection configuration can not be combined with retry or timeout configuration on the same virtual service <Cite n={11} />. Istio failure recovery features are completely transparent to the application <Cite n={11} />. If you also set failure recovery policies in your application code, both work independently and therefore might conflict <Cite n={11} />.

**Mutual TLS and identity.** Peer authentication is used for service-to-service authentication to verify the client making the connection <Cite n={12} />. Istio offers mutual TLS as a full stack solution for transport authentication, which can be enabled without requiring service code changes <Cite n={12} />. With mutual TLS, Istio re-routes the outbound traffic from a client to the client's local sidecar Envoy <Cite n={12} />. The client side Envoy starts a mutual TLS handshake with the server side Envoy <Cite n={12} />. The server side Envoy authorizes the request <Cite n={12} />. Istio configures TLSv1_2 as the minimum TLS version for both client and server <Cite n={12} />.

**Permissive mode.** Istio mutual TLS has a permissive mode, which allows a service to accept both plaintext traffic and mutual TLS traffic at the same time <Cite n={12} />. The docs say that this feature greatly improves the mutual TLS onboarding experience <Cite n={12} />. The operator can gradually install and configure the client's Istio sidecars to send mutual TLS traffic <Cite n={12} />. Once the configuration of the clients is complete, the operator can configure the server to mutual TLS only mode <Cite n={12} />.

**Authorization policies.** Authorization policies support ALLOW, DENY, and CUSTOM actions <Cite n={12} />. Istio checks for matching policies in layers, in this order: CUSTOM, DENY, and then ALLOW <Cite n={12} />. When you apply multiple authorization policies to the same workload, Istio applies them additively <Cite n={12} />. The deny policy takes precedence over the allow policy <Cite n={12} />. An authorization policy includes a selector, an action, and a list of rules <Cite n={12} />. If the selector is not set, the policy applies to all workloads in the same namespace as the policy <Cite n={12} />. The "deny by default" behavior applies only if the workload has at least one authorization policy with the ALLOW action <Cite n={12} />. The docs call it a good security practice to start with the allow-nothing policy and incrementally add more ALLOW policies to open more access to the workload <Cite n={12} />.

**Telemetry.** Istio generates detailed telemetry for all service communications within a mesh <Cite n={13} />. It generates metrics, distributed traces, and access logs <Cite n={13} />. The four golden signals of monitoring are latency, traffic, errors, and saturation <Cite n={13} />. The standard Istio metrics are exported to Prometheus by default <Cite n={13} />. Istio supports distributed tracing through the Envoy proxies <Cite n={13} />. The proxies automatically generate trace spans on behalf of the applications they proxy, requiring only that the applications forward the appropriate request context <Cite n={13} />. Istio supports a number of tracing backends, including Zipkin, Jaeger, and many tools and services that support OpenTelemetry <Cite n={13} />. By default, Istio enables only a small subset of the Envoy-generated statistics <Cite n={13} />.

**Profiles.** The built-in deployment profiles are just sets of Helm values overrides <Cite n={14} />. The default profile enables components according to the default settings of the IstioOperator API <Cite n={14} />. The demo profile is designed to showcase Istio functionality with modest resource requirements <Cite n={14} />. The demo profile enables high levels of tracing and access logging, so it is not suitable for performance tests <Cite n={14} />. The minimal profile is the same as the default profile, but only the control plane components are installed <Cite n={14} />. The ambient profile is designed to help you get started with ambient mode <Cite n={14} />. The empty profile deploys nothing <Cite n={14} />. The `istioctl manifest generate` command generates an Istio install manifest and outputs it to the console by default <Cite n={16} />.

**Checking the configuration: `istioctl analyze`.** `istioctl analyze` is a diagnostic tool that can detect potential issues with your Istio configuration <Cite n={15} />. It can run against a live cluster or a set of local configuration files <Cite n={15} />. It can also run against a combination of the two, which allows you to catch problems before you apply changes to a cluster <Cite n={15} />. When you analyze a set of local files, the file-set is expected to be fully self-contained <Cite n={15} />. To analyze local files, add the `--use-kube=false` flag <Cite n={15} />. The reference lists `--all-namespaces` (`-A`) to analyze all namespaces and `--namespace` (`-n`) for one <Cite n={16} />. The `--failure-threshold` option sets the severity level of analysis at which to set a non-zero exit code <Cite n={16} />. The `--output` option takes one of log, json, or yaml, with log as the default <Cite n={16} />. The `-L` option lists the analyzers that are available to run <Cite n={16} />.

**Analysis messages.** The message IST0101 has the level Error, and it occurs when an Istio resource references another resource that does not exist <Cite n={17} />. The docs say that this will lead to errors when Istio tries to look up the referenced resource but cannot find it <Cite n={17} />. For example, a VirtualService refers to a gateway that does not exist <Cite n={17} />. The docs also describe an experimental feature, which is intended for evaluation purposes only <Cite n={15} />. In it, Istio can be set up to perform the analysis alongside the configuration distribution <Cite n={15} />. In that case, the validation messages are written to the status subresource of the affected Istio resource <Cite n={15} />. The docs say that this is not a replacement for `istioctl analyze` <Cite n={15} />.

**Falco: events in, alerts out.** Falco uses syscalls to monitor a system's activity <Cite n={18} />. It parses the Linux syscalls from the kernel at runtime <Cite n={18} />. It asserts the stream against a rules engine, and it alerts when a rule is violated <Cite n={18} />. Falco's monitoring capabilities are not limited to syscalls, because it can be extended via plugins to ingest data from many more types of sources <Cite n={18} />. The userspace program handles signals, parses information from a Falco driver, and sends alerts <Cite n={18} />. The configuration defines how Falco is run, what rules to assert, and how to perform alerts <Cite n={18} />.

**Event sources.** Falco natively supports the syscall event source, which is enabled by default, consuming events from the Linux Kernel via drivers <Cite n={19} />. The plugin system allows Falco to extend its capabilities with new event sources <Cite n={19} />. Each event source operates in an isolated thread <Cite n={19} />. Rules are partitioned by event source, meaning each rule applies to a specific source and is triggered exclusively by events from that source <Cite n={19} />. Falco does not support correlating events from different sources <Cite n={19} />. By default, Falco enables event collection from all known sources <Cite n={19} />. For plugin-based deployments, you disable the syscall source explicitly, or enable only the plugin-based sources that you want <Cite n={19} />. Attempting to disable unknown sources or all sources results in an error <Cite n={19} />.

**Drivers and privileges.** A driver runs in kernelspace <Cite n={20} />. The driver provides the syscall event source <Cite n={20} />. The docs say that there are several supported drivers <Cite n={20} />. They list the modern eBPF probe, which is the default, and the kernel module <Cite n={20} />. The kernel module requires full privileges and cannot run with Linux capabilities <Cite n={20} />. The modern eBPF probe doesn't require a specific kernel version <Cite n={20} />. Usually, all versions at or above 5.8 are enough <Cite n={20} />. The 2 main required features are BPF ring buffer support and a kernel that exposes BTF <Cite n={20} />. The tarball page says that by default Falco consumes events from the Linux Kernel, which requires Falco to be privileged <Cite n={26} />. To use the syscall source without a driver, you run Falco with `-o engine.kind=nodriver` <Cite n={19} />. In that mode Falco continues to process syscall events, but it does not use a driver to capture them <Cite n={19} />.

**Rules files.** A Falco rules file is a YAML file containing mainly three types of elements <Cite n={22} />. Rules are the conditions under which an alert should be generated <Cite n={22} />. A rule is accompanied by a descriptive output string that is sent with the alert <Cite n={22} />. Macros are rule condition snippets that can be re-used inside rules and even other macros <Cite n={22} />. Lists are collections of items that can be included in rules, macros, or other lists <Cite n={22} />. Unlike rules and macros, lists cannot be parsed as filtering expressions <Cite n={22} />. A rules file can also hold `required_engine_version`, which tracks compatibility between rules content and the Falco engine version <Cite n={22} />.

**A rule.** A rule is a YAML object, part of the rules file, whose definition contains at least the fields `rule`, `desc`, `condition`, `output`, and `priority` <Cite n={23} />. The key part of a rule is the condition field <Cite n={23} />. A condition is a Boolean predicate expressed using the condition syntax <Cite n={23} />. A rule output is a string that can use the same fields that conditions can use, prepended by `%` to perform interpolation, akin to printf <Cite n={23} />. Every Falco rule has a priority which indicates how serious a violation of the rule is <Cite n={23} />. The available priorities are EMERGENCY, ALERT, CRITICAL, ERROR, WARNING, NOTICE, INFORMATIONAL, and DEBUG <Cite n={23} />. In a rule, the priority has nothing to do with overriding another rule or choosing the order in which rules will be triggered <Cite n={23} />. The way to control that order is to change the order in which the rules are defined, and therefore loaded <Cite n={23} />.

**Conditions and event types.** The docs give an example of a condition that alerts whenever a bash shell is run inside a container, `container.id != host and proc.name = bash` <Cite n={23} />. Since this condition does not include a clause with a system call, it will only check event metadata <Cite n={23} />. Because of that, if a bash shell does start up in a container, Falco outputs events for every syscall that is performed by that shell <Cite n={23} />. To be alerted only for each successful spawn of a shell in a container, you add the appropriate event types to the condition <Cite n={23} />. In the example of the docs, if the connection happens outside a container, the field `%container.image.repository` would not be set, and `<NA>` is displayed instead <Cite n={23} />.

**First match wins.** The Falco docs say that Falco by default applies rules per event type on a "first match wins" basis <Cite n={24} />. Starting from Falco Release 0.36.0, you can change the configuration to `rule_matching: all` <Cite n={24} />. This change ensures that rules sharing the same event type cannot override each other, which prevents inconsistent logging <Cite n={24} />. The docs warn that this modification may lead to increased CPU usage <Cite n={24} />.

**Command line.** The `-c` option sets the configuration file, and the default is `/etc/falco/falco.yaml` <Cite n={25} />. The `-V` (`--validate`) option reads the specified rules files, validates the loaded rules, and exits <Cite n={25} />. You can pass it several times to validate several files <Cite n={25} />. The `--dry-run` option runs Falco without processing events, and it can help check that the configuration and rules do not have any errors <Cite n={25} />. The `-L` option shows the name and description of all rules and exits <Cite n={25} />. The `-r` option sets a rules file or directory to be loaded, and only files with a `.yml` or `.yaml` extension are considered <Cite n={25} />. The `-o` option sets the value of a configuration option, and it overrides the values in the configuration file <Cite n={25} />. The `--list` option lists all defined fields and exits <Cite n={25} />.

**Plugins, falcoctl, and outputs.** Plugins are shared libraries that conform to a documented API <Cite n={21} />. They can add new event sources that can be evaluated using filtering expressions or Falco rules <Cite n={21} />. They can add the ability to define new fields that can extract information from events <Cite n={21} />. The `k8s_audit` source is provided by the `k8saudit` plugin <Cite n={19} />. The docs say that the plugins for this source all share the same set of rules <Cite n={19} />. Falcoctl allows you to easily install rules and plugins and perform administrative tasks with Falco <Cite n={18} />. It is bundled together with Falco <Cite n={18} />. Alerts are configurable downstream actions that can be as simple as logging to stdout or as complex as delivering an HTTP request to an endpoint <Cite n={18} />.

**The tarball.** Falco runs on Linux and is available for the x86_64 and aarch64 architectures <Cite n={26} />. Falco with its bundled plugins requires GLIBC 2.28 or newer <Cite n={26} />. The tarball page installs Falco by unpacking the archive and copying it to `/` <Cite n={26} />. The Falco `.tar.gz` archive doesn't include the Systemd setup <Cite n={26} />. The configuration file is located at `/etc/falco/falco.yaml` <Cite n={26} />. You can also override the default configuration by passing options to the falco binary <Cite n={26} />. The tutorial did not copy the archive to `/`. It unpacked the archive in the run folder and passed `-c` and `-o`.

**Rules, maturity levels, and the `event-generator`.** The Falco rules repository says that the `maturity_stable` rules in the `falco_rules.yaml` file are included in the Falco release package <Cite n={40} />. Other maturity-level rules are released separately, and they require explicit installation and possible customization <Cite n={40} />. The repository adds that the Rules Maturity Framework is a best effort on the part of the community, and that you have to decide if any rules are useful for your use cases <Cite n={40} />. The `event-generator` tool generates a variety of suspect actions that are detected by Falco rulesets <Cite n={39} />. Its `k8saudit` events collection is maintained on a best-effort basis and may not fully work <Cite n={39} />.

**OPA: policy apart from the service.** The OPA docs say that today policy is often a hard-coded feature of the software service that it governs <Cite n={28} />. OPA lets you decouple policy from that software service, so that the people responsible for policy can read, write, analyze, version, distribute, and manage it separately from the service <Cite n={28} />. Software services should allow policies to be specified declaratively, updated at any time without recompiling or redeploying, and enforced automatically <Cite n={28} />. Authorization is sometimes confused with authentication <Cite n={28} />.

**Rego.** Rego is declarative, so policy authors can focus on what queries should return rather than how queries should be executed <Cite n={29} />. All modules contain implicit statements which import the data and input documents <Cite n={29} />. Expressions that refer to undefined values are also undefined <Cite n={29} />. The `default` keyword allows policies to define a default value for documents produced by rules with complete definitions <Cite n={29} />. The `with` keyword allows queries to programmatically specify values nested under the input document or the data document, or built-in functions <Cite n={29} />. Variables in Rego are existentially quantified by default <Cite n={29} />.

**Rego v1 and v0.** The OPA CLI reference lists the options `--v0-compatible` and `--v1-compatible` <Cite n={31} />. For `opa fmt`, v1 Rego is formatted to v1, and v0 Rego is rejected <Cite n={31} />. With `opa fmt --v0-compatible`, v0 Rego is formatted to v0, and v1 Rego is rejected <Cite n={31} />. The Gatekeeper docs say that using Rego v1 syntax in a ConstraintTemplate is opt-in, and that by default only Rego v0 is allowed <Cite n={37} />.

**Testing policies.** OPA gives you a framework that you can use to write tests for your policies <Cite n={30} />. Tests are expressed as standard Rego rules with a convention that the rule name is prefixed with `test_` <Cite n={30} />. The `opa test` subcommand runs all of the tests found in Rego files passed on the command line <Cite n={30} />. If the test rule is undefined or generates a non-true value, the test result is reported as FAIL <Cite n={30} />. If the test encounters a runtime error, the result is marked as an ERROR <Cite n={30} />. Tests prefixed with `todo_` are reported as SKIPPED <Cite n={30} />. When you misspell a test name or run no test by accident, `opa test` still succeeds <Cite n={30} />. The docs say that `--fail-on-empty` is also useful in CI/CD pipelines to ensure that tests are actually being executed <Cite n={30} />. `opa test` can also report coverage for the policies under test <Cite n={30} />.

**The command line.** The `opa eval` option `--fail` exits with a non-zero exit code on an undefined or empty result and on errors <Cite n={31} />. The option `--fail-defined` exits with a non-zero exit code on a defined or non-empty result and on errors <Cite n={31} />. `opa check` checks Rego source files for parse and compilation errors <Cite n={31} />. If it succeeds, it produces no output <Cite n={31} />. If it fails, it outputs the errors and exits with a non-zero exit code <Cite n={31} />. The reference lists `--strict`, which enables compiler strict mode <Cite n={31} />. `opa fmt --fail` returns a non-zero exit code if a file would be reformatted <Cite n={31} />. `opa fmt -l` outputs the names of files that would change if formatted <Cite n={31} />.

**The server and the REST API.** The Data API takes `POST /v1/data/{path}` with a JSON body of the form `{"input": ...}` <Cite n={32} />. The request body contains an object that specifies a value for the input document <Cite n={32} />. Any additional top-level keys in the request body beyond `input` are treated as request metadata <Cite n={32} />. The server returns 200 if the path refers to an undefined document <Cite n={32} />. In that case, the response does not contain a `result` property <Cite n={32} />. If decision logging is enabled, the response has a `decision_id` field that uniquely identifies the decision <Cite n={32} />. Callers can use the identifier for correlation purposes <Cite n={32} />.

**Decision logs.** OPA can report decision logs to remote HTTP servers, using custom plugins, or to the console output, or to any combination of these <Cite n={33} />. The decision logs contain events that describe policy queries <Cite n={33} />. Each event includes the policy that was queried, the input to the query, bundle metadata, and other information that enables auditing and offline debugging of policy decisions <Cite n={33} />. When decision logging is enabled, the OPA server includes a `decision_id` field in API calls that return policy decisions <Cite n={33} />. The `path` field of an event holds the hierarchical policy decision path <Cite n={33} />. The `input` field holds the input data provided in the policy query <Cite n={33} />. The `result` field holds the policy decision returned to the client <Cite n={33} />.

**OPA in Kubernetes admission control.** With plain OPA, the Kubernetes API Server is configured to query OPA for admission control decisions when objects are created, updated, or deleted <Cite n={34} />. The API Server sends the entire Kubernetes object in the webhook request to OPA <Cite n={34} />. OPA evaluates the policies it has loaded using the admission review as input <Cite n={34} />. In that input, `input.request.object` contains the entire Kubernetes object <Cite n={34} />. Gatekeeper adds an extensible, parameterized policy library <Cite n={34} />. It also adds audit functionality <Cite n={34} />. Plain OPA and Kube-mgmt are alternatives that can be reached for if you want to use the management features of OPA, such as status logs, decision logs, and bundles <Cite n={34} />.

**Gatekeeper constraint templates.** ConstraintTemplates are made of two main elements <Cite n={37} />. One is the Rego or CEL policy code that defines the policy violation logic <Cite n={37} />. The other is the schema of the accompanying Constraint object, which represents an instantiation of a ConstraintTemplate <Cite n={37} />. The Gatekeeper docs show a `violation` rule that has a `msg` <Cite n={37} />. The docs say that Rego v1 syntax can only be used under `targets[_].code[_]` with `engine: Rego` and `version: "v1"` <Cite n={37} />.

**gator.** The gator CLI is a tool for evaluating Gatekeeper ConstraintTemplates and Constraints in a local environment <Cite n={38} />. `gator test` tests a set of Kubernetes objects against a set of Templates and Constraints <Cite n={38} />. The command returns violations when found and communicates success or failure via its exit status <Cite n={38} />. `gator verify` organizes tests into three levels: Suites, Tests, and Cases <Cite n={38} />. A Case defines an object to validate and whether the object is expected to pass validation <Cite n={38} />. The value of `violations` is either "yes", "no", or a non-negative integer <Cite n={38} />. If it is a non-negative integer, exactly that many violations must match <Cite n={38} />. The `message` field is a regular expression used to match the violation message <Cite n={38} />. To be valid, a Suite file must declare `kind: Suite` and an `apiVersion` <Cite n={38} />. `gator verify` silently ignores files which do not declare these <Cite n={38} />. The docs note that audit and `gator test` are different enforcement points, and that they do not have the AdmissionReview request metadata <Cite n={38} />.

**OPA with Envoy and Istio.** Envoy supports an External Authorization filter which calls an authorization service to check if the incoming request is authorized or not <Cite n={35} />. The OPA-Envoy plugin extends OPA with a gRPC server that implements the Envoy External Authorization API <Cite n={35} />. You can use it to enforce fine-grained, context-aware access control policies with Envoy without modifying your microservice <Cite n={35} />. The plugin has a `dry-run` option <Cite n={35} />. Its default is `false` <Cite n={35} />.

**OPA with Terraform.** OPA makes it possible to write policies that check the changes Terraform is about to make before it makes them <Cite n={36} />. OPA policies operate on the JSON plan generated by Terraform, which has certain limitations <Cite n={36} />. Some information may not be available at plan time <Cite n={36} />. The docs name unknown values, dynamic blocks, and function calls <Cite n={36} />. Before writing policies, you confirm that the information you need is available in the plan JSON output <Cite n={36} />. [Terraform and Infrastructure as Code](/devops/terraform-iac/) explains the plan. The tutorial did not run OPA on a Terraform plan.

</Technical>
<Layman>

**Istio: the two planes.** An Istio service mesh is logically split into a data plane and a control plane <Cite n={9} />. In sidecar mode, the data plane is a set of intelligent proxies, Envoy, deployed as sidecars <Cite n={9} />. These proxies mediate and control all network communication between microservices <Cite n={9} />. They also collect and report telemetry on all mesh traffic <Cite n={9} />. Envoy proxies are the only Istio components that interact with data plane traffic <Cite n={9} />. The control plane manages and configures the proxies to route traffic <Cite n={9} />.

**Istiod.** Istiod provides service discovery, configuration, and certificate management <Cite n={9} />. It converts high level routing rules that control traffic behavior into Envoy-specific configurations, and propagates them to the sidecars at runtime <Cite n={9} />. Istiod acts as a Certificate Authority (CA) and generates certificates to allow secure mTLS communication in the data plane <Cite n={9} />. The Istio docs say that the sidecar proxy model allows you to add Istio capabilities to an existing deployment without requiring you to rearchitect or rewrite code <Cite n={9} />.

**Ambient mode.** In ambient mode, Istio implements its features using a per-node Layer 4 proxy, and optionally a per-namespace Layer 7 proxy <Cite n={10} />. This layered approach lets you adopt Istio in a more incremental fashion <Cite n={10} />. Waypoint proxies are heavier than the ztunnel overlay alone <Cite n={10} />. They still run as an ambient component of the infrastructure, and they need no modification of the application pods <Cite n={10} />.

**Traffic routing: virtual services and destination rules.** Virtual services, along with destination rules, are the key building blocks of Istio's traffic routing functionality <Cite n={11} />. A virtual service lets you configure how requests are routed to a service within an Istio service mesh <Cite n={11} />. Each virtual service consists of a set of routing rules that are evaluated in order <Cite n={11} />. A virtual service can send traffic to different versions of a service, such as "20% of calls go to the new version" <Cite n={11} />. This allows a canary rollout, where you gradually increase the percentage of traffic that is sent to a new service version <Cite n={11} />. Destination rules let you customize Envoy's traffic policies when calling the entire destination service or a particular service subset <Cite n={11} />. In some cases you also need to configure destination rules to use the features of virtual services, as destination rules are where you specify your service subsets <Cite n={11} />.

**Gateways.** Gateways are primarily used to manage ingress traffic, but you can also configure egress gateways <Cite n={11} />. An egress gateway lets you configure a dedicated exit node for the traffic leaving the mesh <Cite n={11} />. Istio provides some preconfigured gateway proxy deployments that you can use, istio-ingressgateway and istio-egressgateway <Cite n={11} />. Both are deployed if you use the demo installation <Cite n={11} />. Just the ingress gateway is deployed with the default profile <Cite n={11} />.

**Resilience: timeouts, retries, and circuit breakers.** A timeout is the amount of time that an Envoy proxy should wait for replies from a given service <Cite n={11} />. The Envoy timeout for HTTP requests is disabled in Istio by default <Cite n={11} />. A retry setting specifies the maximum number of times an Envoy proxy attempts to connect to a service if the initial call fails <Cite n={11} />. The default retry behavior for HTTP requests is to retry twice before returning the error <Cite n={11} />. In a circuit breaker, you set limits for calls to individual hosts within a service <Cite n={11} />. Once that limit has been reached, the circuit breaker "trips" and stops further connections to that host <Cite n={11} />. You configure circuit breaker thresholds in destination rules <Cite n={11} />.

**Fault injection.** Fault injection is a testing method that introduces errors into a system to ensure that it can withstand and recover from error conditions <Cite n={11} />. Istio lets you inject faults at the application layer, unlike delaying packets or killing pods at the network layer <Cite n={11} />. You can inject two types of faults, both configured using a virtual service <Cite n={11} />. Delays are timing failures that mimic increased network latency or an overloaded upstream service <Cite n={11} />. Aborts are crash failures that mimic failures in upstream services <Cite n={11} />. Currently, the fault injection configuration can not be combined with retry or timeout configuration on the same virtual service <Cite n={11} />. Istio failure recovery features are completely transparent to the application <Cite n={11} />. If you also set failure recovery policies in your application code, both work independently and therefore might conflict <Cite n={11} />.

**Mutual TLS and identity.** Peer authentication is used for service-to-service authentication to verify the client making the connection <Cite n={12} />. Istio offers mutual TLS as a full stack solution for transport authentication, which can be enabled without requiring service code changes <Cite n={12} />. With mutual TLS, Istio re-routes the outbound traffic from a client to the client's local sidecar Envoy <Cite n={12} />. The client side Envoy starts a mutual TLS handshake with the server side Envoy <Cite n={12} />. The server side Envoy authorizes the request <Cite n={12} />. Istio configures TLSv1_2 as the minimum TLS version for both client and server <Cite n={12} />.

**Permissive mode.** Istio mutual TLS has a permissive mode, which allows a service to accept both plaintext traffic and mutual TLS traffic at the same time <Cite n={12} />. The docs say that this feature greatly improves the mutual TLS onboarding experience <Cite n={12} />. The operator can gradually install and configure the client's Istio sidecars to send mutual TLS traffic <Cite n={12} />. Once the configuration of the clients is complete, the operator can configure the server to mutual TLS only mode <Cite n={12} />.

**Authorization policies.** Authorization policies support ALLOW, DENY, and CUSTOM actions <Cite n={12} />. Istio checks for matching policies in layers, in this order: CUSTOM, DENY, and then ALLOW <Cite n={12} />. When you apply multiple authorization policies to the same workload, Istio applies them additively <Cite n={12} />. The deny policy takes precedence over the allow policy <Cite n={12} />. An authorization policy includes a selector, an action, and a list of rules <Cite n={12} />. If the selector is not set, the policy applies to all workloads in the same namespace as the policy <Cite n={12} />. The "deny by default" behavior applies only if the workload has at least one authorization policy with the ALLOW action <Cite n={12} />. The docs call it a good security practice to start with the allow-nothing policy and incrementally add more ALLOW policies to open more access to the workload <Cite n={12} />.

**Telemetry.** Istio generates detailed telemetry for all service communications within a mesh <Cite n={13} />. It generates metrics, distributed traces, and access logs <Cite n={13} />. The four golden signals of monitoring are latency, traffic, errors, and saturation <Cite n={13} />. The standard Istio metrics are exported to Prometheus by default <Cite n={13} />. Istio supports distributed tracing through the Envoy proxies <Cite n={13} />. The proxies automatically generate trace spans on behalf of the applications they proxy, requiring only that the applications forward the appropriate request context <Cite n={13} />. Istio supports a number of tracing backends, including Zipkin, Jaeger, and many tools and services that support OpenTelemetry <Cite n={13} />. By default, Istio enables only a small subset of the Envoy-generated statistics <Cite n={13} />.

**Profiles.** The built-in deployment profiles are just sets of Helm values overrides <Cite n={14} />. The default profile enables components according to the default settings of the IstioOperator API <Cite n={14} />. The demo profile is designed to showcase Istio functionality with modest resource requirements <Cite n={14} />. The demo profile enables high levels of tracing and access logging, so it is not suitable for performance tests <Cite n={14} />. The minimal profile is the same as the default profile, but only the control plane components are installed <Cite n={14} />. The ambient profile is designed to help you get started with ambient mode <Cite n={14} />. The empty profile deploys nothing <Cite n={14} />. The `istioctl manifest generate` command generates an Istio install manifest and outputs it to the console by default <Cite n={16} />.

**Checking the configuration: `istioctl analyze`.** `istioctl analyze` is a diagnostic tool that can detect potential issues with your Istio configuration <Cite n={15} />. It can run against a live cluster or a set of local configuration files <Cite n={15} />. It can also run against a combination of the two, which allows you to catch problems before you apply changes to a cluster <Cite n={15} />. When you analyze a set of local files, the file-set is expected to be fully self-contained <Cite n={15} />. To analyze local files, add the `--use-kube=false` flag <Cite n={15} />. The reference lists `--all-namespaces` (`-A`) to analyze all namespaces and `--namespace` (`-n`) for one <Cite n={16} />. The `--failure-threshold` option sets the severity level of analysis at which to set a non-zero exit code <Cite n={16} />. The `--output` option takes one of log, json, or yaml, with log as the default <Cite n={16} />. The `-L` option lists the analyzers that are available to run <Cite n={16} />.

**Analysis messages.** The message IST0101 has the level Error, and it occurs when an Istio resource references another resource that does not exist <Cite n={17} />. The docs say that this will lead to errors when Istio tries to look up the referenced resource but cannot find it <Cite n={17} />. For example, a VirtualService refers to a gateway that does not exist <Cite n={17} />. The docs also describe an experimental feature, which is intended for evaluation purposes only <Cite n={15} />. In it, Istio can be set up to perform the analysis alongside the configuration distribution <Cite n={15} />. In that case, the validation messages are written to the status subresource of the affected Istio resource <Cite n={15} />. The docs say that this is not a replacement for `istioctl analyze` <Cite n={15} />.

**Falco: events in, alerts out.** Falco uses syscalls to monitor a system's activity <Cite n={18} />. It parses the Linux syscalls from the kernel at runtime <Cite n={18} />. It asserts the stream against a rules engine, and it alerts when a rule is violated <Cite n={18} />. Falco's monitoring capabilities are not limited to syscalls, because it can be extended via plugins to ingest data from many more types of sources <Cite n={18} />. The userspace program handles signals, parses information from a Falco driver, and sends alerts <Cite n={18} />. The configuration defines how Falco is run, what rules to assert, and how to perform alerts <Cite n={18} />.

**Event sources.** Falco natively supports the syscall event source, which is enabled by default, consuming events from the Linux Kernel via drivers <Cite n={19} />. The plugin system allows Falco to extend its capabilities with new event sources <Cite n={19} />. Each event source operates in an isolated thread <Cite n={19} />. Rules are partitioned by event source, meaning each rule applies to a specific source and is triggered exclusively by events from that source <Cite n={19} />. Falco does not support correlating events from different sources <Cite n={19} />. By default, Falco enables event collection from all known sources <Cite n={19} />. For plugin-based deployments, you disable the syscall source explicitly, or enable only the plugin-based sources that you want <Cite n={19} />. Attempting to disable unknown sources or all sources results in an error <Cite n={19} />.

**Drivers and privileges.** A driver runs in kernelspace <Cite n={20} />. The driver provides the syscall event source <Cite n={20} />. The docs say that there are several supported drivers <Cite n={20} />. They list the modern eBPF probe, which is the default, and the kernel module <Cite n={20} />. The kernel module requires full privileges and cannot run with Linux capabilities <Cite n={20} />. The modern eBPF probe doesn't require a specific kernel version <Cite n={20} />. Usually, all versions at or above 5.8 are enough <Cite n={20} />. The 2 main required features are BPF ring buffer support and a kernel that exposes BTF <Cite n={20} />. The tarball page says that by default Falco consumes events from the Linux Kernel, which requires Falco to be privileged <Cite n={26} />. To use the syscall source without a driver, you run Falco with `-o engine.kind=nodriver` <Cite n={19} />. In that mode Falco continues to process syscall events, but it does not use a driver to capture them <Cite n={19} />.

**Rules files.** A Falco rules file is a YAML file containing mainly three types of elements <Cite n={22} />. Rules are the conditions under which an alert should be generated <Cite n={22} />. A rule is accompanied by a descriptive output string that is sent with the alert <Cite n={22} />. Macros are rule condition snippets that can be re-used inside rules and even other macros <Cite n={22} />. Lists are collections of items that can be included in rules, macros, or other lists <Cite n={22} />. Unlike rules and macros, lists cannot be parsed as filtering expressions <Cite n={22} />. A rules file can also hold `required_engine_version`, which tracks compatibility between rules content and the Falco engine version <Cite n={22} />.

**A rule.** A rule is a YAML object, part of the rules file, whose definition contains at least the fields `rule`, `desc`, `condition`, `output`, and `priority` <Cite n={23} />. The key part of a rule is the condition field <Cite n={23} />. A condition is a Boolean predicate expressed using the condition syntax <Cite n={23} />. A rule output is a string that can use the same fields that conditions can use, prepended by `%` to perform interpolation, akin to printf <Cite n={23} />. Every Falco rule has a priority which indicates how serious a violation of the rule is <Cite n={23} />. The available priorities are EMERGENCY, ALERT, CRITICAL, ERROR, WARNING, NOTICE, INFORMATIONAL, and DEBUG <Cite n={23} />. In a rule, the priority has nothing to do with overriding another rule or choosing the order in which rules will be triggered <Cite n={23} />. The way to control that order is to change the order in which the rules are defined, and therefore loaded <Cite n={23} />.

**Conditions and event types.** The docs give an example of a condition that alerts whenever a bash shell is run inside a container, `container.id != host and proc.name = bash` <Cite n={23} />. Since this condition does not include a clause with a system call, it will only check event metadata <Cite n={23} />. Because of that, if a bash shell does start up in a container, Falco outputs events for every syscall that is performed by that shell <Cite n={23} />. To be alerted only for each successful spawn of a shell in a container, you add the appropriate event types to the condition <Cite n={23} />. In the example of the docs, if the connection happens outside a container, the field `%container.image.repository` would not be set, and `<NA>` is displayed instead <Cite n={23} />.

**First match wins.** The Falco docs say that Falco by default applies rules per event type on a "first match wins" basis <Cite n={24} />. Starting from Falco Release 0.36.0, you can change the configuration to `rule_matching: all` <Cite n={24} />. This change ensures that rules sharing the same event type cannot override each other, which prevents inconsistent logging <Cite n={24} />. The docs warn that this modification may lead to increased CPU usage <Cite n={24} />.

**Command line.** The `-c` option sets the configuration file, and the default is `/etc/falco/falco.yaml` <Cite n={25} />. The `-V` (`--validate`) option reads the specified rules files, validates the loaded rules, and exits <Cite n={25} />. You can pass it several times to validate several files <Cite n={25} />. The `--dry-run` option runs Falco without processing events, and it can help check that the configuration and rules do not have any errors <Cite n={25} />. The `-L` option shows the name and description of all rules and exits <Cite n={25} />. The `-r` option sets a rules file or directory to be loaded, and only files with a `.yml` or `.yaml` extension are considered <Cite n={25} />. The `-o` option sets the value of a configuration option, and it overrides the values in the configuration file <Cite n={25} />. The `--list` option lists all defined fields and exits <Cite n={25} />.

**Plugins, falcoctl, and outputs.** Plugins are shared libraries that conform to a documented API <Cite n={21} />. They can add new event sources that can be evaluated using filtering expressions or Falco rules <Cite n={21} />. They can add the ability to define new fields that can extract information from events <Cite n={21} />. The `k8s_audit` source is provided by the `k8saudit` plugin <Cite n={19} />. The docs say that the plugins for this source all share the same set of rules <Cite n={19} />. Falcoctl allows you to easily install rules and plugins and perform administrative tasks with Falco <Cite n={18} />. It is bundled together with Falco <Cite n={18} />. Alerts are configurable downstream actions that can be as simple as logging to stdout or as complex as delivering an HTTP request to an endpoint <Cite n={18} />.

**The tarball.** Falco runs on Linux and is available for the x86_64 and aarch64 architectures <Cite n={26} />. Falco with its bundled plugins requires GLIBC 2.28 or newer <Cite n={26} />. The tarball page installs Falco by unpacking the archive and copying it to `/` <Cite n={26} />. The Falco `.tar.gz` archive doesn't include the Systemd setup <Cite n={26} />. The configuration file is located at `/etc/falco/falco.yaml` <Cite n={26} />. You can also override the default configuration by passing options to the falco binary <Cite n={26} />. The tutorial did not copy the archive to `/`. It unpacked the archive in the run folder and passed `-c` and `-o`.

**Rules, maturity levels, and the `event-generator`.** The Falco rules repository says that the `maturity_stable` rules in the `falco_rules.yaml` file are included in the Falco release package <Cite n={40} />. Other maturity-level rules are released separately, and they require explicit installation and possible customization <Cite n={40} />. The repository adds that the Rules Maturity Framework is a best effort on the part of the community, and that you have to decide if any rules are useful for your use cases <Cite n={40} />. The `event-generator` tool generates a variety of suspect actions that are detected by Falco rulesets <Cite n={39} />. Its `k8saudit` events collection is maintained on a best-effort basis and may not fully work <Cite n={39} />.

**OPA: policy apart from the service.** The OPA docs say that today policy is often a hard-coded feature of the software service that it governs <Cite n={28} />. OPA lets you decouple policy from that software service, so that the people responsible for policy can read, write, analyze, version, distribute, and manage it separately from the service <Cite n={28} />. Software services should allow policies to be specified declaratively, updated at any time without recompiling or redeploying, and enforced automatically <Cite n={28} />. Authorization is sometimes confused with authentication <Cite n={28} />.

**Rego.** Rego is declarative, so policy authors can focus on what queries should return rather than how queries should be executed <Cite n={29} />. All modules contain implicit statements which import the data and input documents <Cite n={29} />. Expressions that refer to undefined values are also undefined <Cite n={29} />. The `default` keyword allows policies to define a default value for documents produced by rules with complete definitions <Cite n={29} />. The `with` keyword allows queries to programmatically specify values nested under the input document or the data document, or built-in functions <Cite n={29} />. Variables in Rego are existentially quantified by default <Cite n={29} />.

**Rego v1 and v0.** The OPA CLI reference lists the options `--v0-compatible` and `--v1-compatible` <Cite n={31} />. For `opa fmt`, v1 Rego is formatted to v1, and v0 Rego is rejected <Cite n={31} />. With `opa fmt --v0-compatible`, v0 Rego is formatted to v0, and v1 Rego is rejected <Cite n={31} />. The Gatekeeper docs say that using Rego v1 syntax in a ConstraintTemplate is opt-in, and that by default only Rego v0 is allowed <Cite n={37} />.

**Testing policies.** OPA gives you a framework that you can use to write tests for your policies <Cite n={30} />. Tests are expressed as standard Rego rules with a convention that the rule name is prefixed with `test_` <Cite n={30} />. The `opa test` subcommand runs all of the tests found in Rego files passed on the command line <Cite n={30} />. If the test rule is undefined or generates a non-true value, the test result is reported as FAIL <Cite n={30} />. If the test encounters a runtime error, the result is marked as an ERROR <Cite n={30} />. Tests prefixed with `todo_` are reported as SKIPPED <Cite n={30} />. When you misspell a test name or run no test by accident, `opa test` still succeeds <Cite n={30} />. The docs say that `--fail-on-empty` is also useful in CI/CD pipelines to ensure that tests are actually being executed <Cite n={30} />. `opa test` can also report coverage for the policies under test <Cite n={30} />.

**The command line.** The `opa eval` option `--fail` exits with a non-zero exit code on an undefined or empty result and on errors <Cite n={31} />. The option `--fail-defined` exits with a non-zero exit code on a defined or non-empty result and on errors <Cite n={31} />. `opa check` checks Rego source files for parse and compilation errors <Cite n={31} />. If it succeeds, it produces no output <Cite n={31} />. If it fails, it outputs the errors and exits with a non-zero exit code <Cite n={31} />. The reference lists `--strict`, which enables compiler strict mode <Cite n={31} />. `opa fmt --fail` returns a non-zero exit code if a file would be reformatted <Cite n={31} />. `opa fmt -l` outputs the names of files that would change if formatted <Cite n={31} />.

**The server and the REST API.** The Data API takes `POST /v1/data/{path}` with a JSON body of the form `{"input": ...}` <Cite n={32} />. The request body contains an object that specifies a value for the input document <Cite n={32} />. Any additional top-level keys in the request body beyond `input` are treated as request metadata <Cite n={32} />. The server returns 200 if the path refers to an undefined document <Cite n={32} />. In that case, the response does not contain a `result` property <Cite n={32} />. If decision logging is enabled, the response has a `decision_id` field that uniquely identifies the decision <Cite n={32} />. Callers can use the identifier for correlation purposes <Cite n={32} />.

**Decision logs.** OPA can report decision logs to remote HTTP servers, using custom plugins, or to the console output, or to any combination of these <Cite n={33} />. The decision logs contain events that describe policy queries <Cite n={33} />. Each event includes the policy that was queried, the input to the query, bundle metadata, and other information that enables auditing and offline debugging of policy decisions <Cite n={33} />. When decision logging is enabled, the OPA server includes a `decision_id` field in API calls that return policy decisions <Cite n={33} />. The `path` field of an event holds the hierarchical policy decision path <Cite n={33} />. The `input` field holds the input data provided in the policy query <Cite n={33} />. The `result` field holds the policy decision returned to the client <Cite n={33} />.

**OPA in Kubernetes admission control.** With plain OPA, the Kubernetes API Server is configured to query OPA for admission control decisions when objects are created, updated, or deleted <Cite n={34} />. The API Server sends the entire Kubernetes object in the webhook request to OPA <Cite n={34} />. OPA evaluates the policies it has loaded using the admission review as input <Cite n={34} />. In that input, `input.request.object` contains the entire Kubernetes object <Cite n={34} />. Gatekeeper adds an extensible, parameterized policy library <Cite n={34} />. It also adds audit functionality <Cite n={34} />. Plain OPA and Kube-mgmt are alternatives that can be reached for if you want to use the management features of OPA, such as status logs, decision logs, and bundles <Cite n={34} />.

**Gatekeeper constraint templates.** ConstraintTemplates are made of two main elements <Cite n={37} />. One is the Rego or CEL policy code that defines the policy violation logic <Cite n={37} />. The other is the schema of the accompanying Constraint object, which represents an instantiation of a ConstraintTemplate <Cite n={37} />. The Gatekeeper docs show a `violation` rule that has a `msg` <Cite n={37} />. The docs say that Rego v1 syntax can only be used under `targets[_].code[_]` with `engine: Rego` and `version: "v1"` <Cite n={37} />.

**gator.** The gator CLI is a tool for evaluating Gatekeeper ConstraintTemplates and Constraints in a local environment <Cite n={38} />. `gator test` tests a set of Kubernetes objects against a set of Templates and Constraints <Cite n={38} />. The command returns violations when found and communicates success or failure via its exit status <Cite n={38} />. `gator verify` organizes tests into three levels: Suites, Tests, and Cases <Cite n={38} />. A Case defines an object to validate and whether the object is expected to pass validation <Cite n={38} />. The value of `violations` is either "yes", "no", or a non-negative integer <Cite n={38} />. If it is a non-negative integer, exactly that many violations must match <Cite n={38} />. The `message` field is a regular expression used to match the violation message <Cite n={38} />. To be valid, a Suite file must declare `kind: Suite` and an `apiVersion` <Cite n={38} />. `gator verify` silently ignores files which do not declare these <Cite n={38} />. The docs note that audit and `gator test` are different enforcement points, and that they do not have the AdmissionReview request metadata <Cite n={38} />.

**OPA with Envoy and Istio.** Envoy supports an External Authorization filter which calls an authorization service to check if the incoming request is authorized or not <Cite n={35} />. The OPA-Envoy plugin extends OPA with a gRPC server that implements the Envoy External Authorization API <Cite n={35} />. You can use it to enforce fine-grained, context-aware access control policies with Envoy without modifying your microservice <Cite n={35} />. The plugin has a `dry-run` option <Cite n={35} />. Its default is `false` <Cite n={35} />.

**OPA with Terraform.** OPA makes it possible to write policies that check the changes Terraform is about to make before it makes them <Cite n={36} />. OPA policies operate on the JSON plan generated by Terraform, which has certain limitations <Cite n={36} />. Some information may not be available at plan time <Cite n={36} />. The docs name unknown values, dynamic blocks, and function calls <Cite n={36} />. Before writing policies, you confirm that the information you need is available in the plan JSON output <Cite n={36} />. [Terraform and Infrastructure as Code](/devops/terraform-iac/) explains the plan. The tutorial did not run OPA on a Terraform plan.

</Layman>
<Bangla>

**Istio: দুটো plane।** একটা Istio service mesh যৌক্তিকভাবে একটা data plane আর একটা control plane-এ ভাগ করা <Cite n={9} />। sidecar mode-এ data plane হলো sidecar হিসেবে বসানো বুদ্ধিমান proxy-র একটা সেট, Envoy <Cite n={9} />। এই proxy-গুলো microservice-গুলোর মধ্যে সব network যোগাযোগ মধ্যস্থতা আর নিয়ন্ত্রণ করে <Cite n={9} />। তারা mesh-এর সব traffic-এর telemetry-ও সংগ্রহ আর জানায় <Cite n={9} />। data plane traffic-এর সঙ্গে কেবল Envoy proxy-ই যোগাযোগ করে, Istio-র আর কোনো component নয় <Cite n={9} />। control plane traffic routing-এর জন্য proxy-গুলোকে পরিচালনা আর configure করে <Cite n={9} />।

**Istiod।** Istiod service discovery, configuration আর certificate management দেয় <Cite n={9} />। সে traffic আচরণ নিয়ন্ত্রণ করা উচ্চস্তরের routing rule-কে Envoy-নির্দিষ্ট configuration-এ রূপান্তর করে আর runtime-এ sidecar-গুলোতে ছড়িয়ে দেয় <Cite n={9} />। Istiod একটা Certificate Authority (CA)-র মতো কাজ করে আর data plane-এ নিরাপদ mTLS যোগাযোগের জন্য certificate তৈরি করে <Cite n={9} />। Istio docs বলে, sidecar proxy model আপনাকে একটা বিদ্যমান deployment-এ Istio-র সামর্থ্য যোগ করতে দেয়, architecture নতুন করে সাজাতে বা code নতুন করে লিখতে হয় না <Cite n={9} />।

**Ambient mode।** ambient mode-এ Istio তার features চালায় প্রতি node-এ একটা Layer 4 proxy দিয়ে, আর ঐচ্ছিকভাবে প্রতি namespace-এ একটা Layer 7 proxy দিয়ে <Cite n={10} />। এই স্তরভিত্তিক পদ্ধতিতে আপনি Istio ধাপে ধাপে গ্রহণ করতে পারেন <Cite n={10} />। Waypoint proxy শুধু ztunnel overlay-র চেয়ে ভারী <Cite n={10} />। তারা তবু অবকাঠামোর ambient component হিসেবে চলে, আর application pod বদলাতে হয় না <Cite n={10} />।

**Traffic routing: virtual service আর destination rule।** Virtual service আর destination rule হলো Istio-র traffic routing সামর্থ্যের মূল গাঁথুনি <Cite n={11} />। একটা virtual service আপনাকে ঠিক করতে দেয়, একটা Istio service mesh-এর ভেতরে কোনো service-এ request কীভাবে routing হবে <Cite n={11} />। প্রতিটা virtual service-এ থাকে এক সেট routing rule, যেগুলো ক্রমানুসারে মূল্যায়ন হয় <Cite n={11} />। একটা virtual service একটা service-এর বিভিন্ন সংস্করণে traffic পাঠাতে পারে, যেমন "২০% call নতুন সংস্করণে যায়" <Cite n={11} />। এতে একটা canary rollout সম্ভব হয়, যেখানে আপনি নতুন service সংস্করণে পাঠানো traffic-এর শতাংশ ধীরে ধীরে বাড়ান <Cite n={11} />। Destination rule আপনাকে পুরো destination service বা একটা নির্দিষ্ট service subset-কে call করার সময় Envoy-র traffic policy নিজের মতো সাজাতে দেয় <Cite n={11} />। কিছু ক্ষেত্রে virtual service-এর এই বৈশিষ্ট্যগুলো ব্যবহার করতে আপনাকে destination rule-ও সাজাতে হয়, কারণ destination rule-এই আপনি service subset ঠিক করেন <Cite n={11} />।

**Gateway।** Gateway প্রধানত ingress traffic পরিচালনায় ব্যবহৃত হয়, তবে আপনি egress gateway-ও সাজাতে পারেন <Cite n={11} />। একটা egress gateway আপনাকে mesh ছেড়ে যাওয়া traffic-এর জন্য একটা নির্দিষ্ট প্রস্থান node সাজাতে দেয় <Cite n={11} />। Istio কিছু আগে থেকে সাজানো gateway proxy deployment দেয়, যেগুলো আপনি ব্যবহার করতে পারেন, istio-ingressgateway আর istio-egressgateway <Cite n={11} />। demo installation ব্যবহার করলে দুটোই deploy হয় <Cite n={11} />। default profile-এ শুধু ingress gateway deploy হয় <Cite n={11} />।

**Resilience: timeout, retry আর circuit breaker।** timeout হলো একটা নির্দিষ্ট service-এর উত্তরের জন্য Envoy proxy-র যতক্ষণ অপেক্ষা করা উচিত, সেই সময় <Cite n={11} />। Istio-তে HTTP request-এর জন্য Envoy timeout ডিফল্টভাবে বন্ধ <Cite n={11} />। retry setting ঠিক করে, প্রথম call ব্যর্থ হলে Envoy proxy সর্বোচ্চ কতবার service-এ সংযোগের চেষ্টা করবে <Cite n={11} />। HTTP request-এর ডিফল্ট retry আচরণ হলো ত্রুটি ফেরত দেওয়ার আগে দুবার retry করা <Cite n={11} />। circuit breaker-এ আপনি একটা service-এর ভেতরের আলাদা আলাদা host-এ call-এর সীমা ঠিক করেন <Cite n={11} />। সেই সীমায় পৌঁছালে circuit breaker "trip" করে আর ওই host-এ আর সংযোগ দেয় না <Cite n={11} />। আপনি circuit breaker-এর threshold ঠিক করেন destination rule-এ <Cite n={11} />।

**Fault injection।** fault injection হলো এমন একটা পরীক্ষার পদ্ধতি, যা একটা system-এ ত্রুটি ঢোকায়, যাতে নিশ্চিত হওয়া যায় সে ত্রুটির অবস্থা সইতে আর সেখান থেকে সেরে উঠতে পারে <Cite n={11} />। network স্তরে packet দেরি করানো বা pod মেরে ফেলার মতো অন্য উপায়ের উল্টো, Istio আপনাকে application স্তরে fault ঢোকাতে দেয় <Cite n={11} />। আপনি দুই ধরনের fault ঢোকাতে পারেন, দুটোই একটা virtual service দিয়ে সাজানো <Cite n={11} />। Delay হলো সময়ের ব্যর্থতা, যা বাড়ানো network latency বা অতিভারাক্রান্ত upstream service-এর নকল করে <Cite n={11} />। Abort হলো crash-এর ব্যর্থতা, যা upstream service-এর ব্যর্থতার নকল করে <Cite n={11} />। বর্তমানে fault injection configuration একই virtual service-এ retry বা timeout configuration-এর সঙ্গে একত্র করা যায় না <Cite n={11} />। Istio-র failure recovery features application-এর কাছে সম্পূর্ণ স্বচ্ছ <Cite n={11} />। আপনি application code-এও failure recovery policy ঠিক করলে দুটো স্বাধীনভাবে কাজ করে, তাই সংঘাত বাধতে পারে <Cite n={11} />।

**Mutual TLS আর identity।** Peer authentication service-to-service authentication-এর জন্য ব্যবহৃত হয়, সংযোগকারী client-কে যাচাই করতে <Cite n={12} />। Istio transport authentication-এর জন্য mutual TLS দেয় একটা full stack সমাধান হিসেবে, যা service code না বদলেই চালু করা যায় <Cite n={12} />। mutual TLS-এ Istio একটা client-এর বাইরে যাওয়া traffic client-এর স্থানীয় sidecar Envoy-তে ঘুরিয়ে দেয় <Cite n={12} />। client-পক্ষের Envoy server-পক্ষের Envoy-র সঙ্গে একটা mutual TLS handshake শুরু করে <Cite n={12} />। server-পক্ষের Envoy request অনুমোদন করে <Cite n={12} />। Istio client আর server দুই পক্ষের জন্য ন্যূনতম TLS সংস্করণ হিসেবে TLSv1_2 ঠিক করে <Cite n={12} />।

**Permissive mode।** Istio mutual TLS-এর একটা permissive mode আছে, যা একটা service-কে একই সময়ে plaintext traffic আর mutual TLS traffic দুটোই নিতে দেয় <Cite n={12} />। docs বলে, এই feature mutual TLS-এ ওঠার অভিজ্ঞতা অনেক ভালো করে <Cite n={12} />। operator ধীরে ধীরে client-এর Istio sidecar বসিয়ে আর সাজিয়ে সেগুলোকে mutual TLS traffic পাঠানোর উপযোগী করতে পারেন <Cite n={12} />। client-এর configuration শেষ হলে operator server-কে শুধু mutual TLS mode-এ সাজাতে পারেন <Cite n={12} />।

**Authorization policy।** Authorization policy ALLOW, DENY আর CUSTOM action সমর্থন করে <Cite n={12} />। Istio মিলে যাওয়া policy খোঁজে স্তরে স্তরে, এই ক্রমে: CUSTOM, DENY, তারপর ALLOW <Cite n={12} />। আপনি একই workload-এ একাধিক authorization policy প্রয়োগ করলে Istio সেগুলোকে যোগ করে প্রয়োগ করে <Cite n={12} />। deny policy allow policy-র ওপর অগ্রাধিকার পায় <Cite n={12} />। একটা authorization policy-তে থাকে একটা selector, একটা action আর rule-এর একটা তালিকা <Cite n={12} />। selector ঠিক না করা থাকলে policy-টা তার নিজের namespace-এর সব workload-এ প্রযোজ্য হয় <Cite n={12} />। "deny by default" আচরণ কেবল তখনই প্রযোজ্য, যখন workload-এ ALLOW action-সহ অন্তত একটা authorization policy আছে <Cite n={12} />। docs বলে, allow-nothing policy দিয়ে শুরু করে workload-এ আরও বেশি প্রবেশাধিকার খুলতে ধাপে ধাপে আরও ALLOW policy যোগ করা একটা ভালো security অনুশীলন <Cite n={12} />।

**Telemetry।** Istio একটা mesh-এর ভেতরের সব service যোগাযোগের জন্য বিস্তারিত telemetry তৈরি করে <Cite n={13} />। সে metrics, distributed trace আর access log তৈরি করে <Cite n={13} />। monitoring-এর চার "golden signal" হলো latency, traffic, errors আর saturation <Cite n={13} />। Istio-র standard metrics ডিফল্টভাবে Prometheus-এ export হয় <Cite n={13} />। Istio Envoy proxy-র মাধ্যমে distributed tracing সমর্থন করে <Cite n={13} />। proxy-গুলো যে application-গুলোর হয়ে কাজ করে, তাদের হয়ে স্বয়ংক্রিয়ভাবে trace span তৈরি করে, শুধু চায় application যেন উপযুক্ত request context এগিয়ে দেয় <Cite n={13} />। Istio বেশ কিছু tracing backend সমর্থন করে, যার মধ্যে আছে Zipkin, Jaeger আর OpenTelemetry সমর্থন করা অনেক tool ও service <Cite n={13} />। ডিফল্টভাবে Istio Envoy-র তৈরি statistics-এর কেবল একটা ছোট উপসেট চালু রাখে <Cite n={13} />।

**Profile।** built-in deployment profile শুধু Helm values override-এর সেট <Cite n={14} />। default profile IstioOperator API-র default setting অনুযায়ী component চালু করে <Cite n={14} />। demo profile Istio-র কার্যকারিতা দেখানোর জন্য বানানো, সীমিত resource দিয়ে <Cite n={14} />। demo profile উচ্চমাত্রার tracing আর access logging চালু করে, তাই performance test-এর উপযোগী নয় <Cite n={14} />। minimal profile default profile-এর মতোই, কিন্তু শুধু control plane component install হয় <Cite n={14} />। ambient profile ambient mode দিয়ে শুরু করতে সাহায্য করার জন্য বানানো <Cite n={14} />। empty profile কিছুই deploy করে না <Cite n={14} />। `istioctl manifest generate` command একটা Istio install manifest তৈরি করে আর ডিফল্টভাবে console-এ ছাপে <Cite n={16} />।

**configuration পরীক্ষা: `istioctl analyze`।** `istioctl analyze` একটা diagnostic tool, যা আপনার Istio configuration-এর সম্ভাব্য সমস্যা ধরতে পারে <Cite n={15} />। সে একটা চালু cluster-এর বিরুদ্ধে বা স্থানীয় configuration file-এর একটা সেটের বিরুদ্ধে চলতে পারে <Cite n={15} />। সে দুটোর সমন্বয়ের বিরুদ্ধেও চলতে পারে, যাতে cluster-এ পরিবর্তন প্রয়োগের আগেই সমস্যা ধরা যায় <Cite n={15} />। স্থানীয় file-এর একটা সেট বিশ্লেষণ করার সময় file-সেটটা সম্পূর্ণ স্বয়ংসম্পূর্ণ হওয়ার কথা <Cite n={15} />। স্থানীয় file বিশ্লেষণ করতে `--use-kube=false` flag যোগ করুন <Cite n={15} />। reference-এ আছে সব namespace বিশ্লেষণের জন্য `--all-namespaces` (`-A`), আর একটা namespace-এর জন্য `--namespace` (`-n`) <Cite n={16} />। `--failure-threshold` option ঠিক করে বিশ্লেষণের কোন severity স্তরে একটা শূন্য-নয় exit code বসবে <Cite n={16} />। `--output` option নেয় log, json বা yaml-এর একটা, ডিফল্ট log <Cite n={16} />। `-L` option চালানোর জন্য পাওয়া analyzer-গুলোর তালিকা দেয় <Cite n={16} />।

**analysis message।** IST0101 message-এর level Error, আর সে ঘটে যখন একটা Istio resource এমন আরেকটা resource-এর কথা বলে, যা নেই <Cite n={17} />। docs বলে, Istio যখন উল্লিখিত resource খুঁজে পায় না, তখন এটা ত্রুটি ঘটায় <Cite n={17} />। যেমন একটা VirtualService এমন একটা gateway-র কথা বলে, যা নেই <Cite n={17} />। docs একটা পরীক্ষামূলক feature-ও বর্ণনা করে, যা কেবল মূল্যায়নের উদ্দেশ্যে তৈরি <Cite n={15} />। তাতে Istio-কে configuration বিতরণের পাশাপাশি বিশ্লেষণ চালাতে সাজানো যায় <Cite n={15} />। সেক্ষেত্রে validation message প্রভাবিত Istio resource-এর status subresource-এ লেখা হয় <Cite n={15} />। docs বলে, এটা `istioctl analyze`-এর বিকল্প নয় <Cite n={15} />।
