- `falcoctl artifact install` took three names: `k8saudit:0.18.0`, `json:0.7.4`, and `k8saudit-rules:0.18.0`. It used the configuration file of the archive (`--config`), and it put the files in folders of the run (`--plugins-dir`, `--rulesfiles-dir`, `--state-dir`, and `--assets-dir`). The versions are the tutorial's own choice. The Falco docs say that the install command of falcoctl downloads rules files to a configurable directory, which is `/etc/falco` by default <Cite n={43} />.
- In the output, falcoctl resolved the three names to `ghcr.io/falcosecurity/plugins/plugin/k8saudit:0.18.0`, `ghcr.io/falcosecurity/plugins/plugin/json:0.7.4`, and `ghcr.io/falcosecurity/plugins/ruleset/k8saudit:0.18.0`. It pulled three layers of each artifact. For each artifact it printed `Verifying signature for artifact` and `Signature successfully verified!`. The tutorial did not look at how falcoctl checks a signature or at the key that it uses.
- `ls` showed `libjson.so` and `libk8saudit.so` in `plugins`, and `k8s_audit_rules.yaml` in `rules`. The README of the plugin says that the official ruleset depends on the json plugin <Cite n={45} />. The step installs `json` too.
- The last two commands read the rules file. `Disallowed K8s User` is at line 154, and its condition ends with `not ka.user.name in (allowed_k8s_users) and not ka.user.name in (eks_allowed_k8s_users)`. `Create Privileged Pod` is at line 247, and its description is `Detect an attempt to start a pod with a privileged container`. The user `kubernetes-admin` is in the list `allowed_k8s_users` (line 74) and in the list `full_admin_k8s_users` (line 783). The docs say that lists are collections of items that can be included in rules, macros, or other lists <Cite n={22} />. The tutorial did not print the other names of the lists.

**15. Read Kubernetes audit events from a file.** The block writes three audit events as JSON Lines and a Falco configuration, and it runs Falco twice:

```bash
# One Kubernetes audit event, as one line of JSON: the user kubernetes-admin creates a Pod with a privileged container.
cat > event-privileged.json <<'EOF'
{"kind":"Event","apiVersion":"audit.k8s.io/v1","level":"RequestResponse","auditID":"11111111-2222-3333-4444-555555555555","stage":"ResponseComplete","requestURI":"/api/v1/namespaces/default/pods","verb":"create","user":{"username":"kubernetes-admin","groups":["system:authenticated"]},"sourceIPs":["10.0.0.5"],"userAgent":"kubectl/v1.37.2","objectRef":{"resource":"pods","namespace":"default","name":"qa-privileged","apiVersion":"v1"},"responseStatus":{"metadata":{},"code":201},"requestObject":{"kind":"Pod","apiVersion":"v1","metadata":{"name":"qa-privileged","namespace":"default"},"spec":{"containers":[{"name":"c1","image":"busybox:1.36","securityContext":{"privileged":true}}]}},"responseObject":{"kind":"Pod","apiVersion":"v1","metadata":{"name":"qa-privileged","namespace":"default"}},"requestReceivedTimestamp":"2026-11-20T01:40:00.000000Z","stageTimestamp":"2026-11-20T01:40:00.100000Z"}
EOF
# The same event from another user, and the same event without the privileged setting.
sed 's/"username":"kubernetes-admin"/"username":"qa-user"/' event-privileged.json > event-other-user.json
sed 's/"privileged":true/"privileged":false/' event-privileged.json > event-plain.json
cat event-privileged.json event-other-user.json event-plain.json > audit.jsonl
echo "events in audit.jsonl: $(wc -l < audit.jsonl)"

# A small Falco configuration: the two plugins, the rules file, and the audit file as the source of events.
cat > falco-audit.yaml <<EOF
rules_files:
  - $PWD/rules/k8s_audit_rules.yaml
load_plugins: [k8saudit, json]
plugins:
  - name: k8saudit
    library_path: $PWD/plugins/libk8saudit.so
    init_config: ""
    open_params: "$PWD/audit.jsonl"
  - name: json
    library_path: $PWD/plugins/libjson.so
stdout_output:
  enabled: true
EOF

# stdout only (stderr is dropped). sed removes the time at the start of each alert line.
echo "--- the default setting"
falco -c falco-audit.yaml --disable-source syscall 2> /dev/null | sed -E 's/^[0-9:.]+: //'
echo "exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"
echo "--- -o rule_matching=all"
falco -c falco-audit.yaml --disable-source syscall -o rule_matching=all 2> /dev/null | sed -E 's/^[0-9:.]+: //'
echo "exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
events in audit.jsonl: 3
--- the default setting
Warning Pod started with privileged container (user=kubernetes-admin pod=qa-privileged resource=pods ns=default images=(busybox:1.36) init_images=<NA> ephemeral_images=<NA>)
Warning K8s Operation performed by user not in allowed list of users (user=qa-user target=qa-privileged/pods verb=create uri=/api/v1/namespaces/default/pods resp=201)
Events detected: 2
Rule counts by severity:
   WARNING: 2
Triggered rules by rule name:
   Disallowed K8s User: 1
   Create Privileged Pod: 1
exit=0
--- -o rule_matching=all
Warning Pod started with privileged container (user=kubernetes-admin pod=qa-privileged resource=pods ns=default images=(busybox:1.36) init_images=<NA> ephemeral_images=<NA>)
Warning K8s Operation performed by user not in allowed list of users (user=qa-user target=qa-privileged/pods verb=create uri=/api/v1/namespaces/default/pods resp=201)
Warning Pod started with privileged container (user=qa-user pod=qa-privileged resource=pods ns=default images=(busybox:1.36) init_images=<NA> ephemeral_images=<NA>)
Events detected: 3
Rule counts by severity:
   WARNING: 3
Triggered rules by rule name:
   Disallowed K8s User: 1
   Create Privileged Pod: 2
exit=0
```

- `event-privileged.json` is one audit event. The user `kubernetes-admin` creates the Pod `qa-privileged` with a privileged container (verb `create`, resource `pods`, response code 201). `event-other-user.json` is the same event from the user `qa-user`. `event-plain.json` is the same event with `privileged` set to `false`. `audit.jsonl` has the three events, one on each line. The events are the tutorial's own.
- The README of the plugin says that it supports Kubernetes audit events from the Webhook backend or from a file <Cite n={45} />. For a file, the plugin expects JSONL, where each line is a JSON object with one or more audit events <Cite n={45} />. The README says that the expected way to use the plugin with Falco is a Webhook, and that file reading can be used for testing and development <Cite n={45} />. The Falco docs say that, to receive Kubernetes audit events, the plugin embeds a webserver that listens on a configurable port and accepts POST requests on a configurable endpoint <Cite n={47} />. The tutorial read a file and ran no webserver.
- `falco-audit.yaml` is the tutorial's own configuration. It lists the rules file of step 14, `load_plugins: [k8saudit, json]`, the two plugins with their library paths, and the file in `open_params`. The README says that a parameter with no scheme is read as a file path <Cite n={45} />. Its sample has the same `load_plugins` list <Cite n={45} />.
- The `--disable-source` option turns off a specific event source <Cite n={25} />. The step gave it `syscall`. The docs say that for plugin-based deployments, you disable syscall explicitly or enable only the desired plugin-based sources <Cite n={19} />.
- With the default setting, Falco printed two alerts for the three events, and `Events detected: 2`. The first event raised `Pod started with privileged container` for `kubernetes-admin`. The second event raised `K8s Operation performed by user not in allowed list of users` for `qa-user`. The third event raised no alert. The step removed the time at the start of each alert line.
- The second event is a privileged Pod too, and in the default setting it did not raise `Create Privileged Pod`. In the rules file of step 14, `Disallowed K8s User` is defined at line 154 and `Create Privileged Pod` at line 247. The docs say that by default Falco applies rules per event type on a "first match wins" basis <Cite n={24} />. They say that the way to control the order in which rules are triggered is to change the order in which the rules are defined, and therefore loaded <Cite n={23} />. In this run, the alert of the second event came from the rule that is defined first.
- With `-o rule_matching=all`, Falco printed three alerts and `Events detected: 3`. The second event raised both rules, and `Create Privileged Pod` was counted twice. The docs say that from Falco 0.36.0 you can change the configuration to `rule_matching: all` <Cite n={24} />. They say that this change ensures that rules sharing the same event type cannot override each other <Cite n={24} />. They warn that it may lead to increased CPU usage <Cite n={24} />. The tutorial did not measure CPU.
- Falco exited with 0 in both runs, although it raised alerts. In this run, the exit code did not show the alerts.

</Technical>
<Layman>

The tutorial ran steps 1 to 15 on 2026-11-20 at 02:24 (+06) on Linux 7.0.0-43-generic. The versions were istioctl 1.32.0, OPA 1.22.4, gator v3.23.1, Falco 0.45.2, and falcoctl 0.14.3. The helpers were Python 3.14.4, curl 8.18.0, and bash 5.3.9. The output on this page is from the tutorial's run. Each step ran in a subshell of the runner, in one new folder, with a new, empty `HOME`. The page shows the folder of the run as `/work`.

The run needs no cluster, no Docker, and no root rights. `istioctl` reads local files only (`--use-kube=false`). OPA and gator work on local files. The OPA server listens on `127.0.0.1:18181` only. Falco reads audit events from a file, and it loads no kernel driver. Step 14 pulls three artifacts from `ghcr.io`. After the run, the real home folder had no new entry, and the folder of the tools had no new or removed file. The port 18181 was free, and the run left no `opa` or `falco` process. The empty home folder of the run held one entry after the steps: `.sigstore`, with 23 files. The tutorial did not check which command made it.

The choices that no source gives are the tutorial's own. They are the Istio files (the namespace `shop`, the service `reviews`, and the subsets `v1` and `v2`), the policy `pods.rego` and its tests, the Gatekeeper template `qanoprivileged`, the rule `QA write to a sensitive file`, the three audit events, and the port 18181. The tutorial did not run Istio in a cluster. The run has no sidecar, no ambient data plane, no traffic, and no mutual TLS handshake. It did not run the admission webhook or the audit of Gatekeeper in a cluster. It did not run OPA with Envoy or with a Terraform plan. It did not run Falco with a driver. No syscall event was read. It did not run the `event-generator`. It ran on Linux only.

**Install the tools.** The tutorial's install script put the four tools in the home folder of the user, with no `sudo`. It took each archive from the release place of its project. `istioctl` and `gator` came from GitHub releases, OPA from `openpolicyagent.org/downloads`, and Falco from `download.falco.org`. The OPA docs say that checksums for all binaries are available in the download path <Cite n={27} />. The script compared the SHA-256 of the OPA binary with that file. For `istioctl` and `gator`, it compared the SHA-256 of the archive with the checksum file of the same GitHub release. That choice is the tutorial's own.

The tutorial found no checksum file for the Falco archive. The runner asked for ten file names next to the archive on `download.falco.org`, and only the archive and its `.asc` file answered with 200:

```text
200 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz
200 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.asc
404 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.sig
404 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.sha256
404 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.sha256sum
404 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.sha512
404 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.md5
404 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.cert
404 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.pem
404 falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz.bundle
```

The Falco packages page tells you to trust the falcosecurity GPG key <Cite n={41} />. It gives the key file at `https://falco.org/repo/falcosecurity-packages.asc` <Cite n={41} />. The script imported that key into a throw-away GnuPG home and checked the `.asc` file against the archive. The signature was good, and its primary key was `478B2FBBC75F4237B731DA4365106822B35B1B1F`. The script did not compare that fingerprint with a second source. For `istioctl`, OPA, and gator, the checksum file and the archive come from the same host. For Falco, the key comes from `falco.org` and the archive from `download.falco.org`. The tutorial treats these checks as a way to find a damaged or swapped archive, and not as proof of who built it. The record of the install is:

```text
istioctl 1.32.0; sha256 4771175dec92527cd90ddc7edb3b3abcb32ca2f3bc32b31aa31e0b12ad36f897
opa v1.22.4; sha256 b76461cf4db0f77946ef557d727a1da5d7a22ba930844678c9be0bebd2c9d0f6
gator v3.23.1; sha256 c268d7b809c9fe59a110ab0bf7c296ab00b0b6c894c209cd15e94194590291bb
falco 0.45.2; sha256 b2e9961e51601095765c6c644329673e6af2f06c02357d0e77da0f680e4910da; signature good, primary key 478B2FBBC75F4237B731DA4365106822B35B1B1F
```

In your own terminal, put `istioctl`, `opa`, and `gator` on your `PATH`. Unpack the Falco archive in the folder of the practice, and put its `usr/bin` folder on your `PATH`. The steps call `falco` and `falcoctl` by name. The tarball page of the Falco docs unpacks the archive with `tar` and copies it to `/` <Cite n={26} />. The tutorial did not copy anything to `/`. The page also says that Falco with its bundled plugins requires GLIBC 2.28 or newer <Cite n={26} />. The glibc of the tutorial's machine is 2.43.

**1. Check the tools.** Run this in the folder of the practice. The first command unpacks the Falco archive:

```bash
tar -xzf falco-0.45.2-x86_64.tar.gz
istioctl version --remote=false
opa version | head -n 1
gator --version
falco --version
falcoctl version
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
client version: 1.32.0
Version: 1.22.4
gator version v3.23.1 (Feature State: beta), opa/v1.17.1, frameworks/v0.0.0-20260616163050-e1eaa1bf6d62
Falco version: 0.45.2
Libs version:  0.26.1
Plugin API:    3.12.0
Engine:        0.65.0
Driver:
  API version:    11.0.0
  Schema version: 4.3.0
  Default driver: 11.0.1+driver
Client Version: 0.14.3
```

- `istioctl version --remote=false` printed `client version: 1.32.0`. The reference says to use `--remote=false` to suppress the control plane check <Cite n={16} />.
- `opa version` printed `Version: 1.22.4` on its first line. The step cut the output there with `head -n 1`.
- `gator --version` printed `v3.23.1 (Feature State: beta)`, and the same line has `opa/v1.17.1`. The `opa` binary of the run is 1.22.4. The two numbers differ, and the tutorial did not find out what the `opa/v1.17.1` in the gator line stands for.
- `falco --version` printed 0.45.2 with the version of the libs, the plugin API, and the engine. `falcoctl version` printed `Client Version: 0.14.3`.
- These commands print versions only. The Falco docs say that falcoctl is bundled together with Falco <Cite n={18} />.

**2. Render the install manifests of five profiles.** Run:

```bash
for p in default demo minimal ambient empty; do
  istioctl manifest generate --set profile=$p > manifest-$p.yaml
  echo "$p: $(grep -c '^kind:' manifest-$p.yaml) objects; workloads: $(awk '/^kind: (Deployment|DaemonSet)$/ {k=$2; w=1; next} w && /^  name:/ {print k "/" $2; w=0}' manifest-$p.yaml | tr '\n' ' ')"
done
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
default: 39 objects; workloads: Deployment/istio-ingressgateway Deployment/istiod 
demo: 42 objects; workloads: Deployment/istio-egressgateway Deployment/istio-ingressgateway Deployment/istiod 
minimal: 33 objects; workloads: Deployment/istiod 
ambient: 44 objects; workloads: DaemonSet/istio-cni-node DaemonSet/ztunnel Deployment/istiod 
empty: 0 objects; workloads: 
```

- The loop wrote the manifest of each profile to a file and counted the lines that start with `kind:`. The count of objects and the list of workloads are the tutorial's own way to compare profiles.
- The reference says that `istioctl manifest generate` generates an Istio install manifest and outputs to the console by default <Cite n={16} />. The step wrote the output to files, and it applied nothing.
- The `empty` profile gave 0 objects. The docs say that the empty profile deploys nothing <Cite n={14} />.
- The `minimal` profile gave 33 objects, and its only workload is `istiod`. The docs say that the minimal profile is the same as the default profile, but only the control plane components are installed <Cite n={14} />.
- The `default` profile gave 39 objects, with `istio-ingressgateway` and `istiod`. The `demo` profile gave 42 objects, with `istio-egressgateway` added. The traffic management page says that both gateways are deployed with the demo installation, and just the ingress gateway with the default profile <Cite n={11} />.
- The `ambient` profile gave 44 objects. Its workloads are the DaemonSets `istio-cni-node` and `ztunnel`, and `istiod`. The ambient overview names the ztunnel secure overlay as the base of ambient mode <Cite n={10} />.

**3. Check a good Istio file.** The command block writes `mesh-good.yaml`, and then it analyzes the file:

```bash
cat > mesh-good.yaml <<'EOF'
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: shop
  labels:
    istio-injection: enabled
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: reviews
  namespace: shop
spec:
  selector:
    app: reviews
  ports:
    - name: http
      port: 9080
---
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: Gateway
metadata:
  name: shop-gateway
  namespace: shop
spec:
  selector:
    istio: ingressgateway
  servers:
    - port:
        number: 80
        name: http
        protocol: HTTP
      hosts:
        - "shop.example.com"
---
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: reviews
  namespace: shop
spec:
  hosts:
    - reviews
  http:
    - route:
        - destination:
            host: reviews
            subset: v1
          weight: 80
        - destination:
            host: reviews
            subset: v2
          weight: 20
---
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: DestinationRule
metadata:
  name: reviews
  namespace: shop
spec:
  host: reviews
  subsets:
    - name: v1
      labels:
        version: v1
    - name: v2
      labels:
        version: v2
EOF

cat > mesh-bad.yaml <<'EOF'
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: shop
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: reviews
  namespace: shop
spec:
  selector:
    app: reviews
  ports:
    - name: http
      port: 9080
---
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: VirtualService
metadata:
  name: reviews
  namespace: shop
spec:
  hosts:
    - reviews
  gateways:
    - bogus-gateway
  http:
    - route:
        - destination:
            host: reviews
            subset: v3
---
apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1
kind: DestinationRule
metadata:
  name: reviews
  namespace: shop
spec:
  host: reviews
  subsets:
    - name: v1
      labels:
        version: v1
EOF

istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A mesh-good.yaml
echo "exit=$?"
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text

✔ No validation issues found when analyzing mesh-good.yaml.
exit=0
```

- The file has a Namespace `shop` with the label `istio-injection: enabled`, a Service `reviews`, a Gateway, a VirtualService, and a DestinationRule. The VirtualService sends 80 % of the requests to the subset `v1` and 20 % to `v2`. The DestinationRule defines both subsets.
- The file also has the Namespace and the Service that the other objects refer to. The docs say that a set of local files is expected to be fully self-contained <Cite n={15} />.
- `istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A mesh-good.yaml` printed `No validation issues found when analyzing mesh-good.yaml.` and exited with 0. The docs show the same kind of message for a namespace <Cite n={15} />.
- The tutorial's files are its own. They are small, and they are not a model of a real mesh.

**4. Check a bad file, with and without a namespace option.** Step 3 wrote `mesh-bad.yaml` as well. Run:

```bash
echo "--- no namespace option"
istioctl analyze --use-kube=false mesh-bad.yaml
echo "exit=$?"
echo "--- -A (all namespaces)"
istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A mesh-bad.yaml
echo "exit=$?"
echo "--- -n shop"
istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -n shop mesh-bad.yaml
echo "exit=$?"
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
--- no namespace option

✔ No validation issues found when analyzing mesh-bad.yaml.
exit=0
--- -A (all namespaces)
Error [IST0101] (VirtualService shop/reviews mesh-bad.yaml:27) Referenced gateway not found: "bogus-gateway"
Error [IST0101] (VirtualService shop/reviews mesh-bad.yaml:31) Referenced host+subset in destinationrule not found: "reviews+v3"
Warning [IST0132] (VirtualService shop/reviews mesh-bad.yaml:27) one or more host [reviews] defined in VirtualService shop/reviews not found in Gateway shop/bogus-gateway.
Error: Analyzers found issues when analyzing all namespaces.
See https://istio.io/v1.32/docs/reference/config/analysis for more information about causes and resolutions.
exit=79
--- -n shop
Error [IST0101] (VirtualService shop/reviews mesh-bad.yaml:27) Referenced gateway not found: "bogus-gateway"
Error [IST0101] (VirtualService shop/reviews mesh-bad.yaml:31) Referenced host+subset in destinationrule not found: "reviews+v3"
Warning [IST0132] (VirtualService shop/reviews mesh-bad.yaml:27) one or more host [reviews] defined in VirtualService shop/reviews not found in Gateway shop/bogus-gateway.
Error: Analyzers found issues when analyzing namespace: shop.
See https://istio.io/v1.32/docs/reference/config/analysis for more information about causes and resolutions.
exit=79
```

- `mesh-bad.yaml` has a VirtualService that names the gateway `bogus-gateway` and the subset `v3`. Its DestinationRule has the subset `v1` only.
- With no namespace option, `istioctl analyze --use-kube=false mesh-bad.yaml` printed `No validation issues found when analyzing mesh-bad.yaml.` and exited with 0. The tutorial did not find out why. The reference lists `--namespace` with an empty default <Cite n={16} />.
- With `-A`, it printed three messages and `Error: Analyzers found issues when analyzing all namespaces.` The exit code was 79. The docs show the same sentence for one namespace <Cite n={15} />.
- With `-n shop`, it printed the same three messages and `Error: Analyzers found issues when analyzing namespace: shop.` The exit code was 79.
- Each message has a level, a code, the kind and the name of the resource, the file and the line, and a text.
- The two `IST0101` errors name a gateway and a host with a subset that do not exist. The docs say that this message occurs when an Istio resource references another resource that does not exist <Cite n={17} />. The list of messages names it ReferencedResourceNotFound <Cite n={42} />.
- The list of messages names `IST0132` VirtualServiceHostNotFoundInGateway <Cite n={42} />.
- The exit code was 79 in the tutorial's run. The tutorial did not look up the meaning of the number.

**5. Severity, exit codes, and JSON output.** Run:

```bash
# A copy of the good file in which the VirtualService names the gateway shop-gateway, which does not list the host "reviews".
sed 's/^    - reviews$/&\n  gateways:\n    - shop-gateway/' mesh-good.yaml > mesh-warn.yaml
istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A mesh-warn.yaml
for level in Error Warning Info; do
  istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A --failure-threshold $level mesh-warn.yaml > /dev/null 2>&1
  echo "mesh-warn.yaml, --failure-threshold $level: exit=$?"
done
istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A mesh-bad.yaml > /dev/null 2>&1
echo "mesh-bad.yaml, default output: exit=$?"
istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A -o json mesh-bad.yaml > bad.json 2> bad.err
echo "mesh-bad.yaml, -o json: exit=$?"
python3 -c 'import json; print([(m["code"], m["level"]) for m in json.load(open("bad.json"))])'
echo "analyzers: $(istioctl analyze -L | grep -c '^\* ')"
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
Warning [IST0132] (VirtualService shop/reviews mesh-warn.yaml:45) one or more host [reviews] defined in VirtualService shop/reviews not found in Gateway shop/shop-gateway.
mesh-warn.yaml, --failure-threshold Error: exit=0
mesh-warn.yaml, --failure-threshold Warning: exit=79
mesh-warn.yaml, --failure-threshold Info: exit=79
mesh-bad.yaml, default output: exit=79
mesh-bad.yaml, -o json: exit=0
[('IST0101', 'Error'), ('IST0101', 'Error'), ('IST0132', 'Warning')]
analyzers: 54
```

- `mesh-warn.yaml` is `mesh-good.yaml` with two more lines in the VirtualService. They name the gateway `shop-gateway`, which does not list the host `reviews`. The analysis printed one warning, `IST0132`, at line 45.
- The reference says that `--failure-threshold` sets the severity level of analysis at which to set a non-zero exit code <Cite n={16} />. The valid values are Info, Warning, and Error, and the default is `Error` <Cite n={16} />.
- On the file with the warning only, the threshold `Error` gave the exit code 0. The thresholds `Warning` and `Info` gave 79. On `mesh-bad.yaml`, the default threshold gave 79.
- With `-o json`, the exit code for `mesh-bad.yaml` was 0 in the tutorial's run. The file `bad.json` had the same three messages: `IST0101` with the level `Error` twice, and `IST0132` with the level `Warning`. The tutorial did not find out why the exit code was 0. In this run, the exit code of the JSON output did not show the errors, and the level of each message did.
- `istioctl analyze -L` listed 54 analyzers in this version. The reference says that `-L` lists the analyzers available to run <Cite n={16} />. The step counted the lines that start with `* `, which is the tutorial's own way to count.

**6. Security files: mutual TLS and authorization policies.** The block writes `mesh-security.yaml` and a copy with a typo, and it analyzes both:

```bash
cat > mesh-security.yaml <<'EOF'
apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: shop
  labels:
    istio-injection: enabled
---
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: PeerAuthentication
metadata:
  name: default
  namespace: shop
spec:
  mtls:
    mode: STRICT
---
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: allow-frontend-get
  namespace: shop
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: reviews
  action: ALLOW
  rules:
    - from:
        - source:
            namespaces: ["frontend"]
      to:
        - operation:
            methods: ["GET"]
---
apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1
kind: AuthorizationPolicy
metadata:
  name: deny-admin
  namespace: shop
spec:
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: reviews
  action: DENY
  rules:
    - to:
        - operation:
            paths: ["/admin"]
EOF
istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A mesh-security.yaml
echo "exit=$?"

# A copy in which the action of the last policy is misspelled. sed removes the date and time of the log line, so that the output is the same in each run.
sed 's/action: DENY/action: DENYY/' mesh-security.yaml > mesh-security-typo.yaml
istioctl analyze --use-kube=false -A mesh-security-typo.yaml 2>&1 | sed -E 's/^[0-9T:.Z-]+\t//'
echo "exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
Warning [IST0133] (AuthorizationPolicy shop/deny-admin mesh-security.yaml:35) Schema validation warning: configured AuthorizationPolicy will deny all traffic to TCP ports under its scope due to the use of only HTTP attributes in a DENY rule; it is recommended to explicitly specify the port
exit=0
warn	file	error processing mesh-security-typo.yaml[3]: failed parsing JSON for built-in type: unknown value "\"DENYY\"" for enum istio.security.v1beta1.AuthorizationPolicy.Action - skipping
Error(s) adding files: 1 error occurred:
	* errors parsing content "mesh-security-typo.yaml": 1 error occurred:
	* error processing mesh-security-typo.yaml[3]: failed parsing JSON for built-in type: unknown value "\"DENYY\"" for enum istio.security.v1beta1.AuthorizationPolicy.Action




No validation issues found when analyzing all namespaces (but 1 file could not be parsed).
Error: Some files couldn't be parsed.
exit=65
```

- `mesh-security.yaml` has the namespace `shop` with injection enabled, a PeerAuthentication named `default` with the mode `STRICT`, an ALLOW policy named `allow-frontend-get`, and a DENY policy named `deny-admin`. Both policies select the workload with the label `app: reviews`. The ALLOW policy lets in the GET requests from the namespace `frontend`. The DENY policy has the path `/admin`.
- The analysis printed one warning, `IST0133`, for the DENY policy at line 35, and it exited with 0. The list of messages names `IST0133` SchemaWarning <Cite n={42} />. The text of the message says that the policy will deny all traffic to TCP ports under its scope due to the use of only HTTP attributes in a DENY rule. It recommends to specify the port explicitly.
- The security page says that certain fields and conditions are only applicable to HTTP workloads <Cite n={12} />. The `paths` field in the `operation` section is one of them <Cite n={12} />. The page also says that Istio will ignore the HTTP-only fields if you use any of them for a TCP workload <Cite n={12} />. The tutorial sent no traffic. It did not check what the policy does with TCP traffic.
- The ALLOW policy uses the field `namespaces` under `source`. The page says that mutual TLS must be enabled before using any of the following fields in the authorization policy <Cite n={12} />. They are the `principals` and `notPrincipals` fields and the `namespaces` and `notNamespaces` fields, all under the `source` section <Cite n={12} />. The file has the PeerAuthentication in the mode `STRICT` in the same namespace. The analysis printed no message about the ALLOW policy.
- In the copy `mesh-security-typo.yaml`, the action of the DENY policy is `DENYY`. `istioctl` printed a `warn` line. It says that it failed parsing JSON for `mesh-security-typo.yaml[3]`, with an unknown value for the enum of the action, and that it was skipping it. Then it printed `No validation issues found when analyzing all namespaces (but 1 file could not be parsed).` and `Error: Some files couldn't be parsed.` The exit code was 65.
- The line `No validation issues found` was printed although the policy with the typo was not analyzed. Only the last line and the exit code showed the problem.
- The reference has the option `--ignore-unknown`, which does not complain about un-parseable input documents, for cases where analyze should run only on k8s compliant inputs <Cite n={16} />. The tutorial did not use it.

**7. Write a policy and ask OPA.** The block writes the policy `pods.rego` and two Pods as JSON, and it runs `opa eval`:

```bash
cat > pods.rego <<'EOF'
package qa.pods

# A pod is denied when one of its containers runs privileged.
deny contains msg if {
	some c in input.spec.containers
	c.securityContext.privileged == true
	msg := sprintf("container %v must not run privileged", [c.name])
}

default allow := false

allow if count(deny) == 0
EOF

cat > bad-pod.json <<'EOF'
{"apiVersion": "v1", "kind": "Pod", "metadata": {"name": "qa-privileged", "namespace": "default"}, "spec": {"containers": [{"name": "c1", "image": "busybox:1.36", "securityContext": {"privileged": true}}]}}
EOF
sed 's/qa-privileged/qa-plain/; s/"privileged": true/"privileged": false/' bad-pod.json > good-pod.json

opa eval --format pretty -d pods.rego -i bad-pod.json 'data.qa.pods.deny'
opa eval --format pretty -d pods.rego -i bad-pod.json 'data.qa.pods.allow'
opa eval --format pretty -d pods.rego -i good-pod.json 'data.qa.pods.allow'
opa eval --fail-defined -d pods.rego -i bad-pod.json 'data.qa.pods.deny[_]' > /dev/null
echo "--fail-defined, bad pod: exit=$?"
opa eval --fail-defined -d pods.rego -i good-pod.json 'data.qa.pods.deny[_]' > /dev/null
echo "--fail-defined, good pod: exit=$?"
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
[
  "container c1 must not run privileged"
]
false
true
--fail-defined, bad pod: exit=1
--fail-defined, good pod: exit=0
```

- `pods.rego` is the tutorial's own policy. Its rule `deny` is a set with one message for each container that has `privileged == true`. Its rule `allow` is `false` by default, and it is `true` when `deny` is empty. The docs say that the `default` keyword lets a policy define a default value for documents produced by rules with complete definitions <Cite n={29} />.
- The input of the query is the Pod that `-i` gives. The docs say that all modules contain implicit statements which import the data and input documents <Cite n={29} />.
- For `bad-pod.json`, `data.qa.pods.deny` printed a list with one message, `container c1 must not run privileged`, and `data.qa.pods.allow` printed `false`. For `good-pod.json`, `allow` printed `true`.
- The reference says that `--fail-defined` exits with a non-zero exit code on a defined or non-empty result <Cite n={31} />. The query `data.qa.pods.deny[_]` has a result for the bad Pod, and the exit code was 1. For the good Pod it has no result, and the exit code was 0.

**8. Test the policy.** The block writes the tests `pods_test.rego`, then it runs `opa test` on the good tests, on a failing copy, and on a copy with misspelled tests:

```bash
cat > pods_test.rego <<'EOF'
package qa.pods_test

import data.qa.pods

test_privileged_pod_is_denied if {
	not pods.allow with input as {"spec": {"containers": [{"name": "c1", "securityContext": {"privileged": true}}]}}
}

test_plain_pod_is_allowed if {
	pods.allow with input as {"spec": {"containers": [{"name": "c1", "securityContext": {"privileged": false}}]}}
}

test_pod_without_security_context_is_allowed if {
	pods.allow with input as {"spec": {"containers": [{"name": "c1"}]}}
}
EOF

# The run times are removed with sed and the lines are sorted, so that the output is the same in each run.
opa test -v pods.rego pods_test.rego | grep -E 'PASS|FAIL' | sed -E 's/ \([^)]*\)$//' | sort
echo "opa test: exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"

# The same policy with a test that expects the wrong answer.
mkdir failing
cp pods.rego failing/
sed 's/^\tnot pods.allow with input/\tpods.allow with input/' pods_test.rego > failing/pods_test.rego
opa test failing/ | sed -E 's/ \([^)]*\)$//'
echo "opa test failing/: exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"

# A copy in which the tests are misspelled: the prefix is tset_ and not test_.
mkdir misspelled
cp pods.rego misspelled/
sed 's/^test_/tset_/' pods_test.rego > misspelled/pods_test.rego
opa test misspelled/
echo "opa test misspelled/: exit=$?"
opa test --fail-on-empty misspelled/
echo "opa test --fail-on-empty misspelled/: exit=$?"

# Coverage of the policy file by the tests
opa test --coverage pods.rego pods_test.rego | python3 -c 'import json, sys; d = json.load(sys.stdin)["files"]["pods.rego"]; print("pods.rego covered lines:", d["covered_lines"], "not covered:", d.get("not_covered_lines", 0), "coverage:", d["coverage"])'
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
PASS: 3/3
data.qa.pods_test.test_plain_pod_is_allowed: PASS
data.qa.pods_test.test_pod_without_security_context_is_allowed: PASS
data.qa.pods_test.test_privileged_pod_is_denied: PASS
opa test: exit=0
failing/pods_test.rego:5:
data.qa.pods_test.test_privileged_pod_is_denied: FAIL
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
PASS: 2/3
FAIL: 1/3
opa test failing/: exit=2
opa test misspelled/: exit=0
no tests were run
opa test --fail-on-empty misspelled/: exit=1
pods.rego covered lines: 6 not covered: 0 coverage: 100
```

- `pods_test.rego` has three tests. A privileged Pod is not allowed, a plain Pod is allowed, and a Pod with no `securityContext` is allowed. The docs say that tests are standard Rego rules with a name that has the prefix `test_` <Cite n={30} />. Each test sets the input with `with input as`. The `with` keyword lets queries specify values nested under the input document <Cite n={29} />.
- `opa test -v pods.rego pods_test.rego` printed `PASS: 3/3` and one `PASS` line for each test, and it exited with 0. The step removed the run times and sorted the lines.
- In `failing/`, the first test expects the privileged Pod to be allowed. `opa test` printed `FAIL` for that test, with `FAIL: 1/3` and `PASS: 2/3`, and it exited with 2. The docs say that the result of a test is FAIL when the test rule is undefined or generates a non-true value <Cite n={30} />.
- In `misspelled/`, the prefix is `tset_`. `opa test` printed nothing and exited with 0. The docs say that when you misspell a test name or run no test by accident, `opa test` will still succeed, and that `--fail-on-empty` makes it fail <Cite n={30} />. With `--fail-on-empty`, it printed `no tests were run` and exited with 1.
- `opa test --coverage` printed a JSON report. The step read three numbers for `pods.rego` from it: 6 covered lines, 0 lines not covered, and a coverage of 100. The docs say that the coverage report includes all of the lines evaluated and not evaluated in the Rego files provided on the command line <Cite n={30} />.

**9. Check, format, and the old syntax.** Run:

```bash
cat > unused.rego <<'EOF'
package qa.unused

import data.qa.nothing

allow if {
	x := 1
	input.ok == true
}
EOF
opa check unused.rego
echo "opa check: exit=$?"
opa check --strict unused.rego
echo "opa check --strict: exit=$?"

printf 'package qa.fmt\n\nallow if {\ninput.ok == true\n}\n' > unformatted.rego
opa fmt --fail --list unformatted.rego
echo "opa fmt --fail --list, unformatted file: exit=$?"
opa fmt --fail --list pods.rego
echo "opa fmt --fail --list, formatted file: exit=$?"

# A policy in the old (v0) syntax: no "if" before the body, no "contains" in the set rule.
cat > v0.rego <<'EOF'
package qa.v0

deny[msg] {
	c := input.spec.containers[_]
	c.securityContext.privileged == true
	msg := sprintf("container %v must not run privileged", [c.name])
}
EOF
opa eval --format pretty -d v0.rego -i bad-pod.json 'data.qa.v0.deny'
echo "opa eval, v0 file: exit=$?"
opa eval --v0-compatible --format pretty -d v0.rego -i bad-pod.json 'data.qa.v0.deny'
echo "opa eval --v0-compatible, v0 file: exit=$?"
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
opa check: exit=0
2 errors occurred:
unused.rego:3: rego_compile_error: import data.qa.nothing unused
unused.rego:6: rego_compile_error: assigned var x unused
opa check --strict: exit=1
unformatted.rego
unexpected diff
opa fmt --fail --list, unformatted file: exit=2
opa fmt --fail --list, formatted file: exit=0
2 errors occurred:
v0.rego:3: rego_parse_error: `if` keyword is required before rule body
v0.rego:3: rego_parse_error: `contains` keyword is required for partial set rules
opa eval, v0 file: exit=2
[
  "container c1 must not run privileged"
]
opa eval --v0-compatible, v0 file: exit=0
```

- `unused.rego` imports `data.qa.nothing`, and it assigns `x := 1`, and it uses neither. `opa check unused.rego` printed nothing and exited with 0. The reference says that if `check` succeeds in parsing and compiling the files, no output is produced <Cite n={31} />.
- `opa check --strict` printed two errors, `import data.qa.nothing unused` and `assigned var x unused`, and it exited with 1. The reference lists `--strict` as the option that enables the compiler strict mode <Cite n={31} />.
- `opa fmt --fail --list` on an unformatted file printed the file name and `unexpected diff`, and it exited with 2. On `pods.rego` it printed nothing and exited with 0. The reference says that with `--fail`, the command `fmt` returns a non-zero exit code if a file would be reformatted <Cite n={31} />. It says that `-l` outputs the names of files that would change <Cite n={31} />.
- `v0.rego` uses the old syntax. It has no `if` before the rule body and no `contains` in the rule of a set. OPA 1.22.4 printed two parse errors and exited with 2. With `--v0-compatible`, the same file worked, and it printed the message. The reference lists `--v0-compatible` as the option that opts in to the OPA features and behaviors prior to the OPA v1.0 release <Cite n={31} />.
- The Gatekeeper docs say that using Rego v1 syntax in a ConstraintTemplate is opt-in, and that by default only Rego v0 is allowed <Cite n={37} />. The template of step 11 uses the old syntax.

**10. Run the OPA server and read its decision log.** The step starts the server in the background, sends four requests with `curl`, stops the server, and reads the log:

```bash
opa run --server --addr 127.0.0.1:18181 --set decision_logs.console=true --log-level error pods.rego > opa-server.log 2>&1 &
OPA_PID=$!
for i in $(seq 1 50); do curl -s -o /dev/null http://127.0.0.1:18181/health && break; sleep 0.1; done
echo "health: $(curl -s http://127.0.0.1:18181/health)"

echo "--- bad pod, wrapped in the input key"
curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:18181/v1/data/qa/pods -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d "{\"input\": $(cat bad-pod.json)}" | sed -E 's/"decision_id":"[^"]*"/"decision_id":"..."/'
echo
echo "--- good pod, wrapped in the input key"
curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:18181/v1/data/qa/pods/allow -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d "{\"input\": $(cat good-pod.json)}" | sed -E 's/"decision_id":"[^"]*"/"decision_id":"..."/'
echo
echo "--- bad pod, NOT wrapped in the input key"
curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:18181/v1/data/qa/pods/allow -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d "$(cat bad-pod.json)" | sed -E 's/"decision_id":"[^"]*"/"decision_id":"..."/'
echo
echo "--- a path that no policy defines"
curl -s -X POST http://127.0.0.1:18181/v1/data/qa/nothing -H 'Content-Type: application/json' -d '{"input": {}}' | sed -E 's/"decision_id":"[^"]*"/"decision_id":"..."/'
echo

kill $OPA_PID
wait $OPA_PID 2> /dev/null
echo "--- decision log (the console lines of the server)"
python3 -c '
import json
for line in open("opa-server.log"):
    d = json.loads(line)
    print(d["msg"], "| path:", d["path"], "| result:", json.dumps(d.get("result", "undefined")), "| input logged:", "input" in d, "| custom.request_metadata logged:", "request_metadata" in d.get("custom", {}))
'
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
health: {}
--- bad pod, wrapped in the input key
{"decision_id":"...","result":{"allow":false,"deny":["container c1 must not run privileged"]}}

--- good pod, wrapped in the input key
{"decision_id":"...","result":true}

--- bad pod, NOT wrapped in the input key
{"decision_id":"...","result":true,"warning":{"code":"api_usage_warning","message":"'input' key missing from the request"}}

--- a path that no policy defines
{"decision_id":"..."}

--- decision log (the console lines of the server)
Decision Log | path: qa/pods | result: {"allow": false, "deny": ["container c1 must not run privileged"]} | input logged: True | custom.request_metadata logged: False
Decision Log | path: qa/pods/allow | result: true | input logged: True | custom.request_metadata logged: False
Decision Log | path: qa/pods/allow | result: true | input logged: False | custom.request_metadata logged: True
Decision Log | path: qa/nothing | result: "undefined" | input logged: True | custom.request_metadata logged: False
```

- The server listens on `127.0.0.1:18181`. The docs say that local console logging of decisions can be enabled via the `console` config option <Cite n={33} />. They show `decision_logs: console: true` for it <Cite n={33} />. The tutorial gave the same setting with `--set decision_logs.console=true`. It set `--log-level error` too, and the log file held decision log lines only. The step waited for `/health`, which printed `{}`.
- The Data API takes `POST /v1/data/{path}` with a JSON body of the form `{"input": ...}` <Cite n={32} />. The first request sent the bad Pod in `input` to `/v1/data/qa/pods`. The result had both rules: `allow` was `false`, and `deny` had the message. The step replaced each `decision_id` with `...`.
- The second request sent the good Pod to `/v1/data/qa/pods/allow`, and the result was `true`.
- The third request sent the bad Pod as the whole body, with no `input` key. The result was `true`, with the warning `'input' key missing from the request`. The policy allowed a privileged Pod in this request. The REST page says that any additional top-level keys in the request body beyond `input` are treated as request metadata <Cite n={32} />. The tutorial did not trace the query, and it does not say why the policy answered `true`.
- The fourth request asked for a path that no policy defines, and the response had no `result` key. The docs say that the server returns 200 if the path refers to an undefined document <Cite n={32} />. They say that the response will not contain a result property in this case <Cite n={32} />. The step did not print the status code.
- The decision log had four lines, one for each request. The `path` field holds the hierarchical policy decision path <Cite n={33} />. The `input` field holds the input data provided in the policy query <Cite n={33} />. The `result` field holds the policy decision returned to the client <Cite n={33} />.
- The third line had no `input` and had `custom.request_metadata`. The REST page says that request metadata is included in decision log events under the custom.request_metadata field <Cite n={32} />. The fourth line had no `result` key, and the step printed `undefined` for it.
- The step stopped the server with `kill` and `wait`. After the run, the port 18181 was free, and no `opa` process was left.

**11. Test a Gatekeeper template with gator.** The block writes a ConstraintTemplate, a Constraint, two Pods, and three more files, and it runs `gator test` and `gator verify`:

```bash
cat > template.yaml <<'EOF'
apiVersion: templates.gatekeeper.sh/v1
kind: ConstraintTemplate
metadata:
  name: qanoprivileged
spec:
  crd:
    spec:
      names:
        kind: QANoPrivileged
  targets:
    - target: admission.k8s.gatekeeper.sh
      rego: |
        package qanoprivileged

        violation[{"msg": msg}] {
          c := input.review.object.spec.containers[_]
          c.securityContext.privileged == true
          msg := sprintf("container <%v> must not run privileged", [c.name])
        }
EOF

cat > constraint.yaml <<'EOF'
apiVersion: constraints.gatekeeper.sh/v1beta1
kind: QANoPrivileged
metadata:
  name: no-privileged-pods
spec:
  match:
    kinds:
      - apiGroups: [""]
        kinds: ["Pod"]
EOF

cat > bad-pod.yaml <<'EOF'
apiVersion: v1
kind: Pod
metadata:
  name: qa-privileged
  namespace: default
spec:
  containers:
    - name: c1
      image: busybox:1.36
      securityContext:
        privileged: true
EOF
sed 's/qa-privileged/qa-plain/; s/privileged: true/privileged: false/' bad-pod.yaml > good-pod.yaml

gator test -f template.yaml -f constraint.yaml -f bad-pod.yaml
echo "gator test, bad pod: exit=$?"
gator test -f template.yaml -f constraint.yaml -f good-pod.yaml
echo "gator test, good pod: exit=$?"

cat > suite.yaml <<'EOF'
kind: Suite
apiVersion: test.gatekeeper.sh/v1alpha1
tests:
  - name: no-privileged
    template: template.yaml
    constraint: constraint.yaml
    cases:
      - name: privileged-pod
        object: bad-pod.yaml
        assertions:
          - violations: 1
            message: must not run privileged
      - name: plain-pod
        object: good-pod.yaml
        assertions:
          - violations: no
EOF
# A copy in which the second case expects a violation that does not happen.
sed 's/violations: no/violations: yes/' suite.yaml > suite-wrong.yaml
# A file that is not a Suite.
printf 'tests: []\n' > not-a-suite.yaml

# The run times are removed with sed, so that the output is the same in each run.
for f in suite.yaml suite-wrong.yaml not-a-suite.yaml; do
  echo "--- gator verify $f"
  gator verify $f 2>&1 | sed -E 's/\t\([0-9.]+s\)$//; s/\t[0-9.]+s$//'
  echo "exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"
done
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
v1/Pod default/qa-privileged: ["no-privileged-pods"] Message: "container <c1> must not run privileged"
gator test, bad pod: exit=1
gator test, good pod: exit=0
--- gator verify suite.yaml
ok	suite.yaml
PASS

exit=0
--- gator verify suite-wrong.yaml
    --- FAIL: plain-pod
        unexpected number of violations: got 0 violations but want at least 1: got messages []
--- FAIL: no-privileged
FAIL	suite-wrong.yaml
FAIL

Error: FAIL
exit=1
--- gator verify not-a-suite.yaml
PASS

exit=0
```

- `template.yaml` is a ConstraintTemplate named `qanoprivileged` with the kind `QANoPrivileged`. It has one target, `admission.k8s.gatekeeper.sh`, and a Rego rule `violation` in the old syntax. `constraint.yaml` is a Constraint named `no-privileged-pods` that matches Pods. The docs say that ConstraintTemplates are made of two main elements <Cite n={37} />. One is the policy code, and the other is the schema of the Constraint <Cite n={37} />. In the Rego, the object under review is `input.review.object`. The docs list `input.review` as the variable that contains the input request object under review <Cite n={37} />.
- `gator test` tests a set of Kubernetes objects against a set of Templates and Constraints <Cite n={38} />. For the bad Pod, it printed one violation line with the message `container <c1> must not run privileged`, and the exit code was 1. For the good Pod, it printed nothing, and the exit code was 0. The docs say that the command returns violations when found and communicates success or failure via its exit status <Cite n={38} />.
- `suite.yaml` has one test with two cases. The case `privileged-pod` expects `violations: 1` and the message `must not run privileged`. The case `plain-pod` expects `violations: no`. The docs say that `violations` is either "yes", "no", or a non-negative integer <Cite n={38} />. They say that `message` is a regular expression used to match the violation message <Cite n={38} />. `gator verify suite.yaml` printed `ok suite.yaml` and `PASS`, and the exit code was 0.
- In `suite-wrong.yaml`, the second case expects `violations: yes`. `gator verify` printed `FAIL` for the case `plain-pod` with the text `unexpected number of violations: got 0 violations but want at least 1`. It printed `Error: FAIL`, and the exit code was 1.
- `not-a-suite.yaml` is the text `tests: []`. `gator verify` printed `PASS` and exited with 0. The docs say that a valid Suite file must declare `kind: Suite` and an `apiVersion`, and that `gator verify` silently ignores files which do not declare these <Cite n={38} />. The tutorial did not check what `gator verify` did with this file.

**12. Validate Falco rules.** The block prints a few lines of the shipped rules file, validates it with and without the container plugin, writes the tutorial's own rules file, validates it, and lists its rules:

```bash
FALCO_ETC=falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falco
FALCO_PLUGINS=$PWD/falco-0.45.2-x86_64/usr/share/falco/plugins
# The Falco log lines start with a time stamp. sed removes it, so that the output is the same in each run.
# The "System info" line is left out as well.
NOSTAMP='s/^[A-Z][a-z]{2} [A-Z][a-z]{2} +[0-9]+ [0-9:]+ [0-9]{4}: //'

echo "--- what the rules file of the archive asks for (lines 28 to 32)"
sed -n '28,32p' $FALCO_ETC/falco_rules.yaml
echo "--- the load_plugins line of the configuration file of the archive"
grep -n '^load_plugins:' $FALCO_ETC/falco.yaml
echo "--- the rules file of the archive, with the default configuration"
falco -c $FALCO_ETC/falco.yaml -V $FALCO_ETC/falco_rules.yaml 2>&1 | grep -v 'System info' | sed -E "$NOSTAMP"
echo "exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"
echo "--- the rules file of the archive, with the container plugin loaded"
falco -c $FALCO_ETC/falco.yaml -o load_plugins[0]=container -o plugins[0].library_path=$FALCO_PLUGINS/libcontainer.so -V $FALCO_ETC/falco_rules.yaml > shipped.log 2>&1
echo "exit=$?"
echo "log lines from the container plugin, left out below: $(grep -c '\[libs\]' shipped.log)"
grep -v -e '\[libs\]' -e 'System info' shipped.log | sed -E "$NOSTAMP"

cat > qa_rules.yaml <<'EOF'
- list: qa_sensitive_files
  items: [/etc/shadow, /etc/sudoers]

- macro: qa_open_write
  condition: >
    evt.type in (open, openat, openat2) and evt.is_open_write=true and fd.typechar='f'

- rule: QA write to a sensitive file
  desc: A process opened a sensitive file for writing. The rule is the tutorial's own.
  condition: qa_open_write and fd.name in (qa_sensitive_files)
  output: Sensitive file opened for writing (file=%fd.name process=%proc.name command=%proc.cmdline user=%user.name)
  priority: WARNING
  tags: [qa, filesystem]
EOF

echo "--- the tutorial's own rules file"
falco -c $FALCO_ETC/falco.yaml -V qa_rules.yaml 2>&1 | grep -v 'System info' | sed -E "$NOSTAMP"
echo "exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"
echo "--- the rule list"
falco -c $FALCO_ETC/falco.yaml -r qa_rules.yaml -L 2>/dev/null
echo "exit=$?"
```

Output of the tutorial's run:

```text
--- what the rules file of the archive asks for (lines 28 to 32)
- required_engine_version: 0.57.0

- required_plugin_versions:
    - name: container
      version: 0.4.0
--- the load_plugins line of the configuration file of the archive
588:load_plugins: []
--- the rules file of the archive, with the default configuration
Falco version: 0.45.2 (x86_64)
Falco initialized with configuration files:
   falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falco/falco.yaml | schema validation: ok
Validating rules file(s):
   falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falco/falco_rules.yaml
Error: Plugin requirement not satisfied, must load one of: container (>= 0.4.0)
exit=1
--- the rules file of the archive, with the container plugin loaded
exit=0
log lines from the container plugin, left out below: 14
Falco version: 0.45.2 (x86_64)
Falco initialized with configuration files:
   falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falco/falco.yaml | schema validation: ok
Loaded plugin 'container@0.7.6' from file /work/falco-0.45.2-x86_64/usr/share/falco/plugins/libcontainer.so
Validating rules file(s):
   falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falco/falco_rules.yaml
falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falco/falco_rules.yaml: Ok
--- the tutorial's own rules file
Falco version: 0.45.2 (x86_64)

   falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falco/falco.yaml | schema validation: ok

   qa_rules.yaml

----                                               -----------
QA write to a sensitive file                       A process opened a sensitive file for writing. The rule is 
                                                   the tutorial's own.

```

- Lines 28 to 32 of the shipped `falco_rules.yaml` hold `required_engine_version: 0.57.0` and a `required_plugin_versions` entry for the plugin `container` in version `0.4.0`. The docs say that `required_engine_version` is used to track compatibility between rules content and the falco engine version <Cite n={22} />. The Falco of the run has the engine version 0.65.0 (step 1).
- The docs say that rules files can have a top-level field `required_plugin_versions`, to document the plugin versions that they are compatible with <Cite n={46} />. They say that if a plugin is loaded and an entry has a matching name, the loaded version must be semver compatible with the version of the entry <Cite n={46} />.
- `falco -V` with the default configuration printed `Error: Plugin requirement not satisfied, must load one of: container (>= 0.4.0)` and exited with 1. The `falco.yaml` of the archive has `load_plugins: []` at line 588.
- The second command loaded the plugin with two `-o` options. The docs say that any configuration option can be overridden on the command line with `-o/--option key=value` <Cite n={44} />. They say that you can override a specific entry of a list by index, as in `--option key.subkey[0]=value` <Cite n={44} />. The docs say that the `plugins` property defines the set of plugins that Falco can load, and that the `load_plugins` property controls which plugins are loaded when Falco starts <Cite n={46} />.
- With the plugin, Falco printed `Loaded plugin 'container@0.7.6'` and `falco_rules.yaml: Ok`, and it exited with 0. The step left out 14 log lines of the plugin.
- The packages page says that on RHEL 8 / UBI 8 systems with glibc older than 2.34, the container plugin bundled with Falco 0.42 to 0.44.x may fail to load with an undefined symbol, and that Falco 0.45.0 bundles a fixed version <Cite n={41} />. The glibc of the tutorial's machine is 2.43.
- `qa_rules.yaml` is the tutorial's own file. It has a list, a macro, and a rule with the priority `WARNING`. The docs say that a rules file is a YAML file with mainly three types of elements <Cite n={22} />. Falco validated it with `qa_rules.yaml: Ok` and exit code 0. A rule has at least the fields `rule`, `desc`, `condition`, `output`, and `priority` <Cite n={23} />.
- `falco -r qa_rules.yaml -L` printed the name and the description of the rule. The reference says that `-L` shows the name and description of all rules and exits <Cite n={25} />.

**13. Break the rule on purpose.** The block makes three copies of `qa_rules.yaml`, each with one mistake, and it validates each copy with `-V`:

```bash
FALCO_ETC=falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falco

# Three copies of the rules file, each with one mistake.
sed 's/fd.name in (qa_sensitive_files)/fd.nam in (qa_sensitive_files)/' qa_rules.yaml > qa_bad_field.yaml
sed 's/qa_open_write and/qa_open_writes and/' qa_rules.yaml > qa_bad_macro.yaml
sed 's/priority: WARNING/priority: LOUD/' qa_rules.yaml > qa_bad_priority.yaml

# stdout only (stderr is dropped).
for f in qa_bad_field.yaml qa_bad_macro.yaml qa_bad_priority.yaml; do
  echo "--- $f"
  falco -c $FALCO_ETC/falco.yaml -V $f 2> /dev/null
  echo "exit=$?"
done
```

- `qa_bad_field.yaml` has `fd.nam` in place of `fd.name`. Falco printed `Invalid`, then `LOAD_ERR_COMPILE_CONDITION (Error compiling condition): filter_check called with nonexistent field fd.nam`. It printed the condition with a `^` under the place of the error, and the exit code was 1. The docs say that `--list` lists all defined fields, so you can look up the right field name <Cite n={25} />. The tutorial did not run it.
- `qa_bad_macro.yaml` has `qa_open_writes` in place of `qa_open_write`. Falco printed `LOAD_ERR_VALIDATE (Error validating rule/macro/list/exception objects): Undefined macro 'qa_open_writes' used in filter.`, and the exit code was 1. The docs say that macros are rule condition snippets that can be re-used inside rules and even other macros <Cite n={22} />.
- `qa_bad_priority.yaml` has `priority: LOUD`. Falco printed `3 Schema warnings`, `1 Errors`, and `LOAD_ERR_YAML_VALIDATE (Error validating internal structure of YAML file): Invalid priority`, and the exit code was 1. The docs list the available priorities <Cite n={23} />.
- Each output starts with the file name and the word `Invalid`. The step printed stdout only, and it dropped stderr.

**14. Install the audit plugin and its rules with falcoctl.** Run:

```bash
mkdir plugins rules state assets
# The log lines start with a date and a time. sed removes them. The order of the three artifacts can differ from run to run.
falcoctl artifact install k8saudit:0.18.0 json:0.7.4 k8saudit-rules:0.18.0 \
  --config falco-0.45.2-x86_64/etc/falcoctl/falcoctl.yaml \
  --plugins-dir plugins --rulesfiles-dir rules --state-dir state --assets-dir assets \
  --log-format text 2>&1 | sed -E 's/^[0-9-]+ [0-9:]+ //'
echo "exit=${PIPESTATUS[0]}"
ls plugins rules
# The two rules that step 15 triggers (the line numbers show their order in the file), and the lists that have the user kubernetes-admin.
grep -n -A 2 -E '^- rule: (Disallowed K8s User|Create Privileged Pod)$' rules/k8s_audit_rules.yaml
awk '/^- list: /{name=$3} /"kubernetes-admin",/{print "line " NR ": in the list " name}' rules/k8s_audit_rules.yaml
```
